We combine the environments used for typing the head term and the remainder of
the stack using
\triangleright
as we wish to account for sequential uses of a mailbox;
for example, in the term
x\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}V\texttt{]};x\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{n}}\texttt{[}W\texttt{]}
,
x
would
have type
\texttt{!}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\odot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{n}})^{\circ}
.

3.4.2.
Properties.

We can now state some metatheoretical results. We relegate proofs to
Appendix
D
.

Typability is preserved by reduction; the proof is nontrivial since we must do
extensive reasoning about environment combination.

Theorem 3.11 (Preservation).

If
\vdash\mathcal{P}
, and

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{C}
with
\Gamma
reliable, and

\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{D}
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{D}
.

Preservation implies mailbox conformance: the property that a configuration will
never evaluate to a singleton failure guard. To
state mailbox conformance, it is useful to define the notion of a

configuration context

\mathcal{H}::=(\nu a)\mathcal{H}\,\mid\,\mathcal{H}\parallel\mathcal{C}\,\mid\,\llparenthesis\,[~],\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis
, that allows us to focus on a single
thread.

Corollary 3.12 (Mailbox Conformance).

If
\vdash\mathcal{P}
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{C}
with
\Gamma
reliable, then
\mathcal{C}\mathop{\not{\longrightarrow^{*}}}\mathcal{H}[\textbf{{fail}}\;V]
.

Progress.

To prove a progress result for
Pat
, we begin with some auxiliary definitions.

Definition 3.13 (Message set).

A
message set

\mathcal{M}
is a configuration of the form:

{a_{1}}\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}_{1}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{V_{1}}\texttt{]}\parallel\cdots\parallel{a_{n}}\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}_{n}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{V_{n}}\texttt{]}
.
We say that a message set
\mathcal{M}

contains a message

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}

for

a
if
\mathcal{M}\equiv{a}\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{V}\texttt{]}\parallel\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

Next, we classify
canonical forms
, which give us a global view of a
configuration. Every well typed process is structurally congruent to a
canonical form.

Definition 3.14 (Canonical form).

A configuration
\mathcal{C}
is in
canonical form
if it is of the
form:
(\nu a_{1})\cdots(\nu a_{l})(\llparenthesis\,M_{1},\Sigma_{1}\,\rrparenthesis\parallel\cdots\parallel\llparenthesis\,M_{m},\Sigma_{m}\,\rrparenthesis\parallel\mathcal{M})

Definition 3.15 (Waiting).

We say that a term
M
is
waiting on mailbox

a
for a message with tag

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}
,
written
\textsf{waiting}(M,a,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})
,
if

M
can be written
\textbf{{guard}}\,a\,{:}\,E\,\{\mathcal{G}[\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}x\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;y\mapsto N]\}
.

Let

\textsf{fv}(-)
denote the set of free variables in a term
M
or frame stack

\Sigma
.
We can then use canonical forms to characterise a progress
result: either each thread can reduce, has reduced to a value, or is waiting for
a message which has not yet been sent by a different thread.

Theorem 3.16 (Partial Progress).

Suppose
\vdash\mathcal{P}
and
\cdot\vdash_{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{C}
where
\mathcal{C}
is in canonical form:

\mathcal{C}=(\nu a_{1})\cdots(\nu a_{l})(\llparenthesis\,M_{1},\Sigma_{1}\,\rrparenthesis\parallel\cdots\llparenthesis\,M_{m},\Sigma_{m}\,\rrparenthesis\parallel\mathcal{M})

Then for each
M_{i}
, either:

•

there exist
M^{\prime}_{i},\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}
such that

\llparenthesis\,M_{i},\Sigma_{i}\,\rrparenthesis\,\longrightarrow\,\llparenthesis\,M^{\prime}_{i},\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}\,\rrparenthesis
; or

•

M_{i}
is a value and
\Sigma_{i}=\epsilon
; or

•

\textsf{waiting}(M_{i},a_{j},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}_{j})

where
\mathcal{M}
does not contain a message

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}_{j}
for
a_{j}

and
a_{j}\not\in\textsf{fv}(\overrightarrow{G_{i}})\cup\textsf{fv}(\Sigma_{i})
,
where
\overrightarrow{G_{i}}
are the guard clauses of
M_{i}
.

A key consequence of Theorem
3.16
is the absence of
self-deadlocks: since we can only guard on a returnable mailbox, and a
returnable name must be the last occurrence in the thread, it cannot be that the

guard
expression is blocking a send to the same mailbox in the same thread.
As we cannot use dependency graphs (§
2.2
), our progress result
does not rule out inter-process deadlocks.

4.
Algorithmic Typing

Writing a typechecker based on
Pat
’s declarative
typing rules is challenging due to nondeterministic context splits, environment
subtyping, and pattern inclusion.
MC
2

(
Padovani 2018b
)
is a
typechecker for the mailbox calculus, based on a typechecker for
concurrent object usage protocols
(
Padovani 2018c
)
. The
MC
2
type system has, however, not been formalised. We adopt several ideas from
MC
2
, especially algorithmic type combination, and adapt the approach for a programming language.

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\text{Pattern variables}&\alpha,\beta\\
\text{Mailbox patterns}&\gamma,\delta&::=&\mathbb{0}\,\mid\,\mathbb{1}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\,\mid\,\gamma\oplus\delta\\
&&\,\mid&\gamma\odot\delta\,\mid\,{{\star}\gamma}\,\mid\,\alpha\\
\text{Mailbox types}&\varsigma&::=&\texttt{!}\gamma\,\mid\,\texttt{?}\gamma\\
\text{Types}&\pi,\rho&::=&C\,\mid\,\varsigma\\
\text{Usage-ann.\ types}&\tau,\sigma&::=&C\,\mid\,\varsigma^{\eta}\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\text{Augmented type envs.}&\Theta&::=&\cdot\,\mid\,\Theta,x:\tau\\
\text{Nullable type envs.}&\Psi&::=&\Theta\,\mid\,\top\\
\text{Augmented definitions}&D&::=&\textbf{{def}}\>f(\overrightarrow{x:\tau}){:}\>\sigma\>\{M\}\\
\text{Constraints}&\phi&::=&\gamma\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\delta\\
\text{Constraint sets}&\Phi\\
\end{array}

Figure 8
.
Pat
syntax extended for algorithmic typing

Type system overview.

Our algorithmic type system takes a

co-contextual

(
Erdweg et al. 2015
)
approach: rather than taking a type environment
as an
input
to the type-checking algorithm, we produce a type environment
as an
output
. The intuition is that (read bottom-up),

splitting
an environment into two sub-environments is more difficult than

merging
two environments inferred from subexpressions.
We also generate
inclusion constraints
on patterns to be
solved later.

Bidirectional type systems
(
Pierce and Turner 2000
;
Dunfield and Krishnaswami 2022
)
split typing rules into
two classes: those that
synthesise
a type
A
for a term
M

(
\Gamma\vdash M\,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\,A
), and those that

check
that a term
M
has type
A

(
\Gamma\vdash M\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,A
). Bidirectional type systems
are syntax-directed and amenable to implementation.

We use a co-contextual variant of bidirectional typing first introduced
by
Zeilberger 2015
. The main twist is the variable rule, which becomes a

checking
rule and records the given variable-type mapping in the
inferred environment.

4.1.
Algorithmic Type System

Extended syntax and annotation

A key difference in comparison to the declarative type system is the addition of

pattern variables

\alpha
, that act as a placeholder for part of a
pattern and are generated during typechecking.
We can then generate and solve
inclusion constraints

\phi

on patterns. Figure
8
shows the extended syntax used in
the algorithmic system.

Constraints

An important challenge for the algorithmic type system is determining whether one
pattern is
included
within another:
e.g.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\sqsubseteq{{\star}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}}
.
Given that patterns may contain pattern variables, we may need to defer
inclusion checking until more pattern variables are known, so we
introduce inclusion constraints
\gamma\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\delta
which require
that pattern
\gamma
is included in pattern
\delta
.

Unrestrictedness

\textsf{unr}(\tau)\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi

\frac{}{\textsf{unr}(C)\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\emptyset}\par\frac{}{\textsf{unr}(\texttt{!}\gamma^{\circ})\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\{\mathbb{1}\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\gamma\}}

Subtyping

\eta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}

\tau\leq\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi

\frac{}{\eta\leq\eta}\par\frac{}{\bullet\leq\circ}\par\frac{}{C\leq C\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\emptyset}\par\frac{\eta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}}{\texttt{!}\gamma^{\eta_{1}}\leq\texttt{!}\delta^{\eta_{2}}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\delta\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\gamma}\par\frac{\eta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}}{\texttt{?}\gamma^{\eta_{1}}\leq\texttt{?}\delta^{\eta_{2}}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\gamma\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\delta}

Type
join

\varsigma_{1}\fatsemi\varsigma_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\varsigma;\Phi

\tau_{1}\fatsemi\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\sigma;\Phi

\frac{}{\texttt{!}\gamma\fatsemi\texttt{!}\delta\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\texttt{!}(\gamma\odot\delta);\emptyset}\par\frac{\alpha{\text{ fresh}}}{\texttt{!}\gamma\fatsemi\texttt{?}\delta\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\texttt{?}\alpha;\{(\gamma\odot\alpha)\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\delta\}}\par\frac{\alpha{\text{ fresh}}}{\texttt{?}\gamma\fatsemi\texttt{!}\delta\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\texttt{?}\alpha;\{(\delta\odot\alpha)\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\gamma\}}\par\frac{\varsigma_{1}\fatsemi\varsigma_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\varsigma;\Phi}{\varsigma_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\fatsemi\varsigma_{2}^{\eta_{2}}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\varsigma^{{\eta_{1}}\triangleright{\eta_{2}}};\Phi}\par\frac{}{C\fatsemi C\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,C;\emptyset}

Type merge

\varsigma_{1}\sqcap\varsigma_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\varsigma;\Phi

\tau_{1}\sqcap\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\sigma;\Phi

\frac{}{\texttt{!}\gamma\sqcap\texttt{!}\delta\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\texttt{!}(\gamma\oplus\delta);\emptyset}\par\frac{\alpha\text{ fresh}}{\texttt{?}\gamma\sqcap\texttt{?}\delta\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\texttt{?}\alpha;\{\alpha\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\gamma,\alpha\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\delta\}}\par\frac{\varsigma_{1}\sqcap\varsigma_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\varsigma;\Phi}{\varsigma_{1}^{\eta_{1}}\sqcap\varsigma_{2}^{\eta_{2}}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\varsigma^{\textsf{max}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})};\Phi}\par\frac{}{C\sqcap C\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,C;\emptyset}

Figure 9
.
Pat
algorithmic type operations

4.1.1.
Algorithmic type operations

Fig.
9
shows the algorithmic type combination
operators.

Unrestrictedness and subtyping.

The algorithmic unrestrictedness operation
\textsf{unr}(\tau)\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi
states that

\tau
is unrestricted subject to constraints
\Phi
, and the definition
reflects the fact that a type is unrestricted in the declarative system if it is
a base type or a subtype of
\texttt{!}\mathbb{1}^{\circ}
.
Algorithmic subtyping is similar: a base type is a subtype of itself,
and we check that two mailbox types with the same capability are subtypes of
each other by generating a contravariant constraint for a send type,
and a covariant constraint for a receive type.

Algorithmic type join.

Declarative mailbox typing relies on
the subtyping rule to manipulate types into a form where
they can be combined with the type combination operators,
e.g.
,

\texttt{!}E\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\boxplus$}}\texttt{?}(E\odot F)=\texttt{?}F
. The algorithmic type system cannot apply the same technique as it does
not know,
a priori
, the form of each pattern. Instead, the algorithmic

type join
operation allows the combination of two mailbox types irrespective
of their syntactic form. Combining two send types is the same as in the
declarative system, but combining a send type with a receive type (and vice
versa) is more interesting: say we wish to combine
\texttt{!}\gamma
and

\texttt{?}\delta
. In this case, we generate a fresh pattern variable

\alpha
; the result is
\texttt{?}\alpha
along with the constraint that

(\gamma\odot\alpha)\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\delta
: namely, that the send
pattern concatenated with the fresh pattern variable is included in the pattern

\delta
.

As an example, joining
\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}
and

\texttt{?}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{n}}\odot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})
produces a receive mailbox
type
\texttt{?}\alpha
and a constraint
({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\odot\alpha)\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{n}}\odot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})
, for which a valid solution
is
\alpha\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{n}}
, and hence the expected combined type

\texttt{?}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{n}}
.

Algorithmic type merge.

In the declarative type system branching control flow requires that each branch
is typable under the same type environment (using the
T-Subs
rule).
The algorithmic type system instead generates constraints that ensure that each
type is used consistently across branches using the
algorithmic type merge
operation

\tau_{1}\sqcap\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\sigma;\Phi
.
Two base types are merged if they are identical.
In the case of mailbox types, the function takes the maximum usage annotation, so

\textsf{max}({\bullet},\circ)={\bullet}
. It ensures that when
merging two output capabilities the patterns are combined using
pattern disjunction. Conversely merging two input capabilities generates a
new pattern variable that must be included in both merged patterns.

Algorithmic environment combination.

We can extend the algorithmic type operations to type environments; the
(omitted, see Appendix
A
) rules are adaptations of the corresponding
declarative operations. Notably, when combining two
environments used in branching control flow such as conditionals where an
output mailbox
\texttt{!}\gamma
is used in one environment but not the other, the
resulting type is
\texttt{!}(\gamma\oplus\mathbb{1})
to signify the

choice
of not sending on the mailbox name.

Nullable type environments.

Checking a
fail
guard produces a
null
environment
\top
which can
be composed with
any other
type environment, as shown by the following
definition:

Definition 4.1 (Nullable environment combination).

For each combination operator
\mathop{\star}\in\{\fatsemi,\sqcap,+\}

we extend environment combination to nullable type environments,

\Psi_{1}\mathop{\star}\Psi_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi;\Phi
by extending
each environment combination operation with the following rules:

\frac{}{\top\mathop{\star}\top\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\top;\emptyset}\par\frac{}{\top\mathop{\star}\Theta\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\emptyset}\par\frac{}{\Theta\mathop{\star}\top\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\emptyset}

Nullable type environments are a supertype of every defined
type environment:
\Theta\leq\top
.

Constraint generation for programs and definitions

\vdash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\Phi

\vdash_{\mathcal{P}}\textbf{{def}}\>f(\overrightarrow{x:\tau}){:}\>\sigma\>\{M\}\triangleright\Phi

\frac{\mathcal{P}=(\mathcal{S},\overrightarrow{D},M)\hskip 8.50012pt(\vdash_{\mathcal{P}}D_{i}\triangleright\Phi_{i})_{i}\hskip 8.50012ptM\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\mathbf{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\cdot;\,\Phi}{\vdash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\Phi\cup\Phi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi_{n}}\par\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsf{check}(\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{\tau},\Theta)=\Phi_{2}\hskip 8.50012pt\Theta\mathop{-}\overrightarrow{x}=\cdot}{\vdash_{\mathcal{P}}\textbf{{def}}\>f(\overrightarrow{x:\tau}){:}\>\sigma\>\{M\}\triangleright\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}}

Constraint generation (synthesis)

M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi

\frac{c\text{ has base type }C}{c\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;C\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\cdot;\,\emptyset}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TS-Const}\par\frac{}{\textbf{{new}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\texttt{?}\mathbb{1}^{{\bullet}}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\cdot;\,\emptyset}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TS-New}\par\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\mathbf{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi}{\textbf{{spawn}}\;M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\mathbf{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\lceil\Theta\rceil;\,\Phi}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TS-Spawn}\par\frac{\mathcal{P}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})=\overrightarrow{\pi}\hskip 8.50012ptV\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}^{\circ}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime};\,\Phi\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt(W_{i}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\lceil\pi_{i}\rceil\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime}_{i};\,\Phi^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in 1..n}\hskip 8.50012pt\Theta^{\prime}+\Theta^{\prime}_{1}+\ldots+\Theta^{\prime}_{n}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi^{\prime\prime}}{V\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{W}\texttt{]}\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\mathbf{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi\cup\Phi^{\prime}_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\Phi^{\prime}_{n}\cup\Phi^{\prime\prime}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TS-Send}\par\frac{\mathcal{P}(f)={\overrightarrow{\tau}\xrightarrow{}\sigma}\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt(V_{i}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau_{i}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{i};\,\Phi_{i})_{i\in 1..n}\hskip 8.50012pt\Theta_{1}+\ldots+\Theta_{n}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi}{f(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi\cup\Phi_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\Phi_{n}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TS-App}

Constraint generation (checking)

M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi

\frac{}{x\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,x:\tau;\,\emptyset}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TC-Var}\par\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\lfloor T\rfloor\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{1};\,\Phi_{1}\hskip 8.50012ptN\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{2};\,\Phi_{2}\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\textsf{check}(\Theta_{2},x,\lfloor T\rfloor)=\Phi_{3}\hskip 8.50012pt\Theta_{1}\mathop{-}x\fatsemi\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{4}}{\textbf{{let}}\>x{{:T}}=M\>\textbf{{in}}\>N\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi_{4}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TC-Let}\par\frac{\{E\}\;\overrightarrow{G}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi;\,\Phi_{1};\,F\hskip 8.50012ptV\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\texttt{?}F^{{\bullet}}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime};\,\Phi_{2}\hskip 8.50012pt\Psi+\Theta^{\prime}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{3}}{\textbf{{guard}}\,V\,{:}\,E\,\{\overrightarrow{G}\}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}\cup\Phi_{3}\cup\{E\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}F\}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TC-Guard}\par\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\hskip 8.50012pt\tau\leq\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi_{2}}{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TC-Sub}

Environment lookup

\textsf{check}(\Theta,x\vphantom{\overrightarrow{x}},\tau)=\Phi

\textsf{check}(\Theta,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{\tau})=\Phi

\frac{x\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta)\hskip 8.50012pt\textsf{unr}(\tau)\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi}{\textsf{check}(\Theta,x,\tau)=\Phi}\par\frac{\sigma\leq\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi}{\textsf{check}((\Theta,x:\tau),x,\sigma)=\Phi}\par\frac{(\textsf{check}(\Theta,x_{i},\tau_{i})=\Phi_{i})_{i}}{\textsf{check}(\Theta,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{\tau})=\Phi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi_{n}}

Figure 10
.
Pat
algorithmic typing (programs, definitions, and terms)

Figure
10
shows the
Pat
algorithmic
typing of programs, definitions and terms. The key idea is to remain in
checking mode for as long as possible, in order to propagate type information to
the variable rule and construct a type environment.
We write

\Theta\mathop{-}\overrightarrow{x}
for
\{y:\tau\mathop{\mid}y:\tau\in\Theta\wedge y\not\in\overrightarrow{x}\}
.

Synthesis.

Our synthesis judgement has the form

M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi
, which can be read
“synthesise type
\tau
for term
M
under program
\mathcal{P}
, producing type
environment
\Theta
and constraints
\Phi
”.
Here,
M
and
\mathcal{P}
are inputs of the judgement, whereas
\tau
,
\Theta
,
and
\Phi
are outputs.
The checking judgement
M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi

can be read
“check that term
M
has type
\tau
under program
\mathcal{P}
, producing type
environment
\Theta
and constraints
\Phi
”.
Here,
M
,
\mathcal{P}
, and
\tau
are inputs of the judgement, whereas
\Theta

and
\Phi
are outputs.
As in the declarative system we omit the
\mathcal{P}
annotation in the rules for readability.

Rule
TS-Const
assigns a known base type to a constant, and rule

TS-New
synthesises a type
\texttt{?}\mathbb{1}^{{\bullet}}
(analogous to

T-New
); both rules produce an empty environment and constraint set.

Rule
TS-Spawn
checks that the given computation
M
has the unit type,
synthesises type
\mathbf{1}
, and infers a type environment
\Theta
and constraint
set
\Phi
. Like
T-Spawn
in the declarative system, the usability
annotations are masked as usable since usability restrictions are process-local.

Message sending
V\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{W}\texttt{]}
is a side-effecting
operation, and so we synthesise type
\mathbf{1}
. Rule
TS-Send

first looks up the payload types
\overrightarrow{\pi}
in the signature,
and
checks
that message target
V
has mailbox type

\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}^{\circ}
. In performing this check, the type
system will produce environment
\Theta^{\prime}
that contains an entry
mapping the variable in
V
to the desired mailbox type

\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}^{\circ}
.
Next, the algorithm checks each payload value against the
payload type described by the signature. The resulting environment is
the algorithmic disjoint combination of the environments produced by
checking each payload, and the resulting constraint set is the union of all
generated constraints.

Function application is similar: rule
TS-App
looks up the
type signature for function
f
and checks that all arguments have the
expected types. The resulting environment is again the disjoint
combination of the environments, and the constraint set is the union
of all generated constraints.

Checking.

Rule
TC-Var

checks
that a variable
x
has type
\tau
,
producing a type environment
x:\tau
.
The
TC-Let
rule checks that a let-binding

\textbf{{let}}\>x{{:T}}=M\>\textbf{{in}}\>N
has type
\tau
: first, we check that
M

has type
\lfloor T\rfloor
noting that only values of returnable type
may be returned, producing environment
\Theta_{1}
and constraints
\Phi_{1}
.
Next we check that the body
N
has type
\tau
, producing environment

\Theta_{2}
and
\Phi_{2}
.
The next step is to check whether the types of the variable inferred in

\Theta_{2}
corresponds with the annotation. The
check
meta-function
ensures that if
x
is not contained within
\Theta_{2}
, then the type of
x
is
unrestricted; and conversely if
x

is
contained within
\Theta_{2}
, then the
annotation is a subtype of the inferred type as the annotation is a
lower
bound
on what the body can expect of
x
.

Rule
TC-Guard
checks that a guard expression

\textbf{{guard}}\,V\,{:}\,E\,\{\overrightarrow{G}\}
has return type
\tau
. First, the rule
checks that the guard sequence
\overrightarrow{G}
has type
\tau
, producing nullable
environment
\Psi
, constraint set
\Phi_{1}
, and pattern
F
in
pattern normal form. Next, the rule checks that the mailbox name
V
has
type
\texttt{?}F^{{\bullet}}
, producing environment
\Theta^{\prime}
and constraint
set
\Phi_{2}
. Finally, the rule calculates the disjoint combination
of
\Psi

and
\Theta^{\prime}
, producing final environment
\Theta
and constraints
\Phi_{3}
.

Finally, rule
TC-Sub
states that if a term
M
is synthesisable with
type
\tau
, where
\tau
is a subtype of
\sigma
, then
M
is checkable
with type
\sigma
. The resulting environment is that produced by synthesising
the type for
M
, and the resulting constraint set is the union of the
synthesis and subtyping constraints.

Un-annotated
let
expressions.

Although our core calculus assumes an annotation on
let

expressions, this is unnecessary if the let-bound variable is
used in the continuation
N
, or
M
has a synthesisable type.
Specifically,
TC-LetNoAnn1
allows us to check the type of the
continuation and inspect the produced environment for the type of
x
, which
can be used to check
M
.
Similarly,
TC-LetNoAnn2
allows us to type a

let
-binding where
x
is
not
used in the continuation,
as long as the type of
M
is synthesisable and unrestricted.

\frac{N\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{1},x:\tau;\,\Phi_{1}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsf{returnable}(\tau)\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptM\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{2};\,\Phi_{2}\hskip 8.50012pt\Theta_{2}\fatsemi\Theta_{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{3}}{\textbf{{let}}\>x{}=M\>\textbf{{in}}\>N\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}\cup\Phi_{3}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TC-LetNoAnn1}\par\frac{N\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{1};\,\Phi_{1}\hskip 8.50012ptx\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{1})\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptM\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{2};\,\Phi_{2}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsf{returnable}(\tau)\hskip 8.50012pt\Theta_{2}\fatsemi\Theta_{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{3}}{\textbf{{let}}\>x{}=M\>\textbf{{in}}\>N\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}\cup\Phi_{3}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TC-LetNoAnn2}

We use the explicitly-typed representation in the core calculus for
simplicity and uniformity, however the implementation follows the above
approach to avoid needless annotations.

Guards.

Constraint generation for guards

\{E\}\;\overrightarrow{G}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi;\,\Phi;\,F

\{E\}\;G\vphantom{\overrightarrow{G}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi;\,\Phi;\,F

\frac{(\{E\}\;G_{i}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi_{i};\,\Phi_{i};\,F_{i})_{i\in 1..n}\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptF={F_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus F_{n}}\hskip 8.50012pt\Psi_{1}\sqcap\ldots\sqcap\Psi_{n}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi;\Phi}{\{E\}\;\overrightarrow{G}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi;\,\Phi\cup\Phi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi_{n};\,F}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TCG-Guards}\par\frac{}{\{E\}\;\textbf{{fail}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\top;\,\emptyset;\,\mathbb{0}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TCG-Fail}\par\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi}{\{E\}\;\textbf{{free}}\mapsto M\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi;\,\mathbb{1}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TCG-Free}\par\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime},y:\texttt{?}\gamma^{{\bullet}};\,\Phi_{1}\hskip 8.50012pt\mathcal{P}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})={\overrightarrow{\pi}}\hskip 8.50012pt\Theta=\Theta^{\prime}\mathop{-}\overrightarrow{x}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsf{base}(\overrightarrow{\pi})\vee\textsf{base}(\Theta)\hskip 8.50012pt\textsf{check}(\Theta^{\prime},\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{\lceil\pi\rceil})=\Phi_{2}}{\{E\}\;\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{x}\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;y\mapsto M\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}\cup\{E\mathop{/}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\gamma\};\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\odot(E\mathop{/}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{TCG-Recv}

Figure 11
.
Pat
algorithmic typing (guards)

Figure
11
shows the typing rules for
guards; the judgement

\{E\}\;G\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi;\,\Phi;\,F
can be read
“Check that guard
G
has type
\tau
,
producing environment
\Psi
, constraints
\Phi
, and
closed pattern literal
F
in pattern normal form with respect to
E
”.
Rule
TCG-Guards
types a
guard sequence, producing the algorithmic merge of all environments and the
sum of all produced patterns. Rule
TCG-Fail
types the
fail
guard
with any type and produces a null type environment, empty constraint set, and
pattern
\mathbb{0}
. Rule
TCG-Free
checks that guard
\textbf{{free}}\mapsto M
has
type
\tau
by checking that
M
has type
\tau
; the guard produces
pattern
\mathbb{1}
.

Finally, rule
TCG-Recv
checks that a receive guard

\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{x}\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;y\mapsto M
has type
\tau
. First, the rule checks that

M
has type
\tau
, producing environment
\Theta^{\prime},y:\texttt{?}\gamma^{{\bullet}}
and constraint set
\Phi_{1}
; since a
mailbox type with input capability is linear, it
must
be present in the
inferred environment. Next, the rule checks that the inferred types for
\overrightarrow{x}

in
\Theta^{\prime}
are compatible with the payloads for
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}
declared in the
signature, producing constraint set
\Phi_{2}
.
As with the declarative rule, to rule out unsafe aliasing either the
payloads or inferred environment must consist only of base types. The resulting
environment is
\Theta
(i.e., the inferred environment without the mailbox
variable or any payloads).
The resulting constraint set is the union of
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
along
with an additional constraint which ensures that
E\mathop{/}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}
is
included in
\gamma
, allowing us to produce the closed PNF literal

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\odot(E\mathop{/}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})
.

4.2.
Metatheory

We can now establish that the algorithmic type system is sound and complete with
respect to the declarative type system. We begin by introducing the notion of
pattern substitutions and solutions.

A
pattern substitution

\Xi
is a mapping from type variables
\alpha
to
(fully-defined) patterns
E
; applying
\Xi
to a pattern
\gamma

substitutes all occurrences of a type variable
\alpha
for
\Xi(\alpha)
.
We extend application of pattern substitutions to types and environments.
We write
\textsf{pv}(E)
for the set of pattern variables in a pattern and extend it to
types and environments.

Definition 4.2 (Pattern solution).

A pattern substitution
\Xi
is a
pattern solution
for a
constraint set
\Phi
(or
solves

\Phi
) if

\textsf{pv}(\Phi)\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
and
for each
\gamma\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\delta\in\Xi
, we have
that
\Xi(\gamma)\sqsubseteq\Xi(\delta)
.
A solution
\Xi
is a
usable
solution if its range does not
contain any pattern equivalent to
\mathbb{0}
.

4.2.1.
Algorithmic soundness

Definition 4.3 (Covering solution).

We say that a pattern substitution
\Xi
is a
covering solution
for a
derivation
M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi
or

M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi
if given

\vdash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\Phi^{\prime}
, it is the case that
\Xi
is a usable
solution for
\Phi\cup\Phi^{\prime}
such that
\textsf{pv}(\tau)\cup\textsf{pv}(\mathcal{P})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
.

If a term is well typed in the algorithmic system then, given a covering
solution, the term is also well typed in the declarative system.

Theorem 4.4 (Algorithmic Soundness).

•

If
\Xi
is a covering solution for

M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi
, then

\Xi(\Theta)\vdash_{\Xi(\mathcal{P})}M\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)
.

•

If
\Xi
is a covering solution for

M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi
, then

\Xi(\Theta)\vdash_{\Xi(\mathcal{P})}M\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)
.

4.2.2.
Algorithmic completeness

We also obtain a completeness result, but only for the checking direction.
This is because the type system
requires
type information to construct a
type environment.
In practice the lack of a completeness result for synthesis is unproblematic
since all functions have return type annotations, and therefore the only terms
typable in the declarative system but unsynthesisable are top-level terms
containing free variables.
In the following we assume that program
\mathcal{P}
is
closed
, i.e. no
definitions or message payloads contain type variables.

Theorem 4.5 (Algorithmic Completeness).

If
\vdash\mathcal{P}
where
\mathcal{P}
is closed, and

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{P}}M\,{:}\,A
,
then there exist some
\Theta,\Phi

and usable solution
\Xi
of
\Phi
such that

M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;A\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi
where
\Gamma\leq\Xi(\Theta)
.

An unannotated
let
binding
\textbf{{let}}\>x{}=M\>\textbf{{in}}\>N
is also
typable by the algorithmic type system if either
x
occurs free in
N
, or
the type of
M
is synthesisable; in practice this encompasses both base
types and linear usages of mailbox types,
i.e.
the vast majority of use
cases.

4.3.
Constraint solving

Constraint solving is covered in depth
by
Padovani 2018c
, so we provide an informal overview:

Identify and group bounds:

A
pattern bound
is of the form
\gamma\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\alpha

i.e.
a constraint whose right-hand-side is a pattern variable.
We firstly group all pattern bounds using pattern disjunction,

e.g.
a constraint set
\{\gamma\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\alpha,\delta\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\alpha,\mathbb{1}\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\mathbb{1}\}
would
result in the constraint

\gamma\oplus\delta\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\alpha
.

Calculate closed-form solutions:

Hopkins and Kozen 1999
define a closed-form solution for a set of pattern
bounds
(\gamma_{i}\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\alpha_{i})_{i}
:
there exists a solution
\delta_{i}
for each
\gamma_{i}
such that

\alpha_{i}\not\in\textsf{pv}(\delta_{i})
.
We can then substitute each closed pattern through the system to
eliminate all pattern variables in the remaining constraints and obtain a
system of closed inclusion constraints.

Translate to Presburger formulae and check satisfiability:

Finally, we translate the closed constraints into Presburger formulae.
Commutative regular expressions, and therefore patterns, can be
expressed as semilinear sets
(
Parikh 1966
)
that describe
Presburger formulae
(
Ginsburg and Spanier 1966
)
.
Since checking the satisfiability of a Presburger formula is decidable,
an external solver like Z3
(
de Moura and Bjørner 2008
)
can be used to
determine whether each constraint holds. In our case, we use Z3’s
quantifier elimination pass and its quantifier-free linear
integer arithmetic solver.

\begin{array}[]{l}\\
\textbf{{guard}}\,\textit{mb}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Arg}}\odot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Arg}}\,\{\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Arg}}\texttt{[}x\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;\textit{mb}^{\prime}\mapsto\\
\hskip 17.00024pt\textbf{{guard}}\,\textit{mb}^{\prime}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Arg}}\,\{\\
\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 8.50012pt\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Arg}}}}\texttt{[}y\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;\textit{mb}^{\prime\prime}\mapsto\\
\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\textbf{{free}}\;\textit{mb}^{\prime\prime};\\
\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024ptx{+}y\\
\hskip 17.00024pt\}\\
\}\\
\end{array}

(a)

Nested
guard
s

\begin{array}[]{l}\textbf{{let}}\>(x,\textit{mb}^{\prime}){}=\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\textbf{{guard}}\,\textit{mb}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Arg}}\odot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Arg}}\,\{\\
\hskip 17.00024pt\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Arg}}}}\texttt{[}x\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;\textit{mb}^{\prime}\mapsto\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 17.00024pt{(x,\textit{mb}^{\prime})}\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\}\;\textbf{{in}}\\
\textbf{{guard}}\,\textit{mb}^{\prime}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Arg}}\,\{\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Arg}}}}\texttt{[}y\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;\textit{mb}^{\prime\prime}\mapsto\\
\hskip 17.00024pt{\textbf{{free}}\;mb^{\prime\prime};\;x{+}y}\\
\}\\
\end{array}

(b)

Unnesting using products

\begin{array}[]{l}\textbf{{guard}}\,\textit{self}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{M1}}\odot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{M2}}\,\{\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{M1}}\texttt{[}\textit{x}\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;\textit{mb}^{\prime}\mapsto\\
\hskip 17.00024pt{\begin{array}[]{l}\textbf{{guard}}\,\textit{mb}^{\prime}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{M2}}\,\{\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{M2}}\texttt{[}\textit{y}\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;\textit{mb}^{\prime\prime}\mapsto\\
\hskip 17.00024pt\textit{x}\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Go}}\texttt{[}\texttt{]};\\
\hskip 17.00024pt\textit{y}\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Go}}\texttt{[}\texttt{]};\\
\hskip 17.00024pt\textbf{{free}}\;\textit{mb}^{\prime\prime}\\
\}\end{array}}\\
\}\end{array}

(c)

Term requiring interfaces

Figure 12
.
Examples motivating extensions

5.
Extensions

It is straightforward to extend
Pat
with product and sum types, and by
using contextual typing information prior to constraint generation, we
can add higher-order functions and
interfaces
that allow finer-grained alias
analysis. The formalisation can be found in Appendix
B
.

5.1.
Product and Sum Types

Product and sum constructors are checking cases, and must contain only
returnable components since we must be able to safely substitute their contents
in any context. As with
let
expressions we can omit annotations on
elimination forms,
i.e.

\textbf{{let}}\>(x,y){}=M\>\textbf{{in}}{N}
or
\textbf{{case}}\;V\;\textbf{{of}}\;\{x\mapsto M;y\mapsto N\}
, provided that
x
and
y
are used in their
continuations, or the sum or product consists of base types.

An advantage of adding product types is that we can avoid nested

guard
clauses, as we can return both a received value and an updated
mailbox name. Figure
12(a)
receives two integers and
returns their sum using nested
guard
expressions, whereas
Figure
12(b)
uses products instead.

Since product types can only contain returnable components, they cannot be used
to replace
n
-ary argument sequences in function definitions and

receive
clauses.

5.2.
Using Contextual Type Information

A co-contextual approach is required to generate the pattern
inclusion constraints. Sometimes, however, it is useful to have
contextual type information
before
the constraint generation pass.
Consider applying a first-class function:

(\lambda^{\!}(x:\textsf{Int}){:}\,\textsf{Int}\,.\,x)(5)
.
Although the annotated
\lambda
expression allows us to synthesise a type
and use a rule similar to
TS-App
, the lack of contextual type
information means that the approach fails as soon as we
stray from applying function literals
as in
\textbf{{let}}\>f{}=(\lambda^{\!}(x:\textsf{Int}){:}\,\textsf{Int}\,.\,x)\>\textbf{{in}}\>f(5)
).
A typical backwards bidirectional typing approach requires

synthesising
function argument types, but this is too
inflexible in our setting as each mailbox name argument would need a type
annotation.

In the base system a global signature maps
message tags to payload types. While technically convenient, this is inflexible.
First, distinct entities may wish to use the same mailbox tags with different
payload types. For example, a client may send a
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Login}}
message
containing credentials to a server,
which may then send a
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{Login}}
message containing the credentials and a
timestamp to a session management server.
Second, we need a syntactic check on a
receive
guard
to avoid aliasing, as outlined in §
2
: either the
received payloads or free variables in the guard body must be base types.
This conservative check rules out innocuous cases such as in
Figure
12(c)
, which waits for messages from two actors
before signalling them to continue.

With contextual information we can associate each mailbox name with an

interface

I
, which maps tags to payload types,
and allows us to syntactically distinguish different kinds of mailboxes
(
e.g.
a future and its client).
Since a name cannot have two interfaces at once, we can loosen our syntactic
check on
receive
guards to require only that the
interfaces
of
mailbox names in the payloads and free variables differ, as typing guarantees
that they will refer to different mailboxes.

We implement the above extensions via a contextual type-directed translation:
we annotate function applications with the type of the
function (
i.e.

{V}^{\overrightarrow{\tau}\xrightarrow{}\sigma}(\overrightarrow{W})
) which
allows us to synthesise the function type.
Users specify an interface when creating a mailbox (
\textbf{{new}}\!\texttt{[}{I}\texttt{]}
);
our pass then annotates sends and guards with interface information (
i.e.

{V}\,{!}^{I}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{W}\texttt{]}
and

\textbf{{guard}}^{I}\,V\,{:}\,E\,\{\overrightarrow{G}\}
) for use in constraint generation.

Typechecking concurrent actor examples in

6.
Implementation and Expressiveness

We outline the implementation of a prototype type checker written in OCaml,
and evidence the expressiveness of
Pat
via a selection of example
programs taken from the literature.
We first show that using quasi-linear typing in place of
dependency graphs (cf. §
2.3
) does not prevent
Pat
from expressing
all
of the examples in
(
de’Liguoro and Padovani 2018
)
.
The Savina benchmarks
(
Imam and Sarkar 2014
)
capture typical
concurrent communication patterns and are used both to compare actor
languages and to demonstrate expressiveness; we show that
Pat
can express
10 of the 11 Savina expressiveness benchmarks used by
Neykova and Yoshida 2017b
.
Finally, we encode a case study provided by an industrial partner that develops control software for factories.

6.1.
Implementation Overview

Pat
programs are type checked in a six-phase pipeline:
lexing and parsing; desugaring, which expands the sugared form of guards (
i.e.
rewrites
\textbf{{free}}\;V
as
\textbf{{guard}}\,V\,{:}\,\mathbb{1}\,\{\textbf{{free}}\mapsto()\}
and

\textbf{{fail}}\;V
as
\textbf{{guard}}\,V\,{:}\,\mathbb{0}\,\{\textbf{{fail}}\}
) and adds omitted
pattern variables; IR conversion, which transforms the surface language
(supporting nested expressions) to our explicitly-sequenced intermediate
representation; contextual type-checking, which supports the contextual
extensions from §
5.2
; constraint
generation; and constraint solving.

The
Pat
typechecker operates in two modes that determine how receive guards are type checked.

Strict
mode uses the lightweight syntactic checks outlined in
§
3
and §
4
, whereas
interface
mode uses interface type information (§
5.2
) to relax these checks.
This means that every
Pat
program accepted in strict mode is also
accepted in interface mode.
More details are given in
Appendix

C
.

6.2.
Expressiveness and Typechecking Time

Table

1
lists the
examples
implemented in
Pat
. Examples 1-6 are the mailbox calculus
examples from
(
de’Liguoro and Padovani 2018
, Ex. 1–3, and Sec. 4.1–4.3)
.
Examples 7-16 are the selection of Savina benchmarks
(
Imam and Sarkar 2014
, Table 1, No.
1–4, 6, 7, 12, 14–16)
used in
(
Neykova and Yoshida 2017b
)
.
The table indicates whether a
Pat
program can be checked in strict (denoted by
\bullet
),
in addition
to interface mode (denoted by
\circ
).
We report the mean typechecking time, excluding phases 1–3 of the pipeline. Measurements are made on a MacBook M1 Pro with 16GB of memory, running macOS 13.2 and OCaml 5.0, and averaging over 1000 repetitions.

Figure 13
.
Factory use case

6.2.1.
Benchmarks

Table

1
shows that all but
one of the mailbox calculus examples from
(
de’Liguoro and Padovani 2018
)

can be checked in strict mode.
The Savina examples
capture typical concurrent programming patterns, namely, master-worker (K-Fork, Fibonacci, Log Map), client-server (Ping Pong, Counter), and peer-to-peer (Big), and common network topologies such as star (Philosopher, Smokers, Transaction) and ring (Thread Ring).
Most of these programs require contextual type information (8, 9, and 12–16) to type check.
As
Pat
does not yet support recursive types, we instead emulate fixed
collections using definition parameters in examples 8, 10, 12–16.
We could not encode the Sleeping Barber
(
Neykova and Yoshida 2017b
, Ex. 8)
example since
the number of collection elements varies throughout execution.

The examples reveal the benefits of mailbox typing.
Runtime checks, such as
manual error handling
(§
1.2
) are unnecessary since errors (
e.g.
unexpected
messages) are
statically
ruled out
by the type system.
Mailbox types also have an edge over session typing tools for actor systems,
e.g.

(
Neykova and Yoshida 2017b
;
Tabone and Francalanza 2022
)

where developers typically specify protocols in external tools and write code to accommodate the session typing framework.
In contrast, mailbox typing
naturally
fits idiomatic actor programming.

This flexibility does not incur high typechecking runtime (see

Table

1
).
The aim of benchmarking typechecking time is to show that mailbox typechecking is not
prohibitively expensive, rather than
to claim comparative results.
Comparisons with other implementations of (non-mailbox-typed versions of) the
benchmarks written in other languages are unlikely to strengthen our results as
the benchmark source code would be different, and we would be measuring
e.g.
Java’s entire type system implementation rather than the essence of the
typechecking algorithm.

6.2.2.
Case Study

Finally we describe a real-world use case written by
Actyx
AG
3
3

3

https://www.actyx.com
, who develop control software for factories. The use case
captures a scenario where multiple robots on a factory floor acquire parts from
a warehouse that provides access through a single door.
Robots negotiate with the door to gain entry into the warehouse and obtain the part they require.
The behaviour of our three entities,
Robot
,
Door
, and

Warehouse
is shown in
Figure

13
.
Our concrete syntax closely follows the core calculus of
§
3
, without requiring that pattern variables in mailbox
types are specified explicitly.
Type checking our completed case study given in Appendix
C.2
relies on contextual type information
(see §
5
), and takes

\approx
89.6 ms.

We give an excerpt of our Warehouse process (below) that maps the interactions of its
lifeline in
Figure

.

.

\lst

\lst

message from the Door and notifies the Robot via a
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle

8
–

).
Subsequent interactions that the Warehouse undertakes with the Door and Robot
are detailed in Appendix
C.2
.

precise
with respect to the messages mailboxes receive.
Specifically, the guard on

at most

exactly
