7.
Related work

Behaviourally-typed actors.

The asymmetric nature of mailboxes makes developing
behavioural type systems for actor languages challenging.

Mostrous and Vasconcelos 2011
investigate session typing for Core Erlang, using selective
message reception and unique references to encode session-typed channels.

Tabone and Francalanza 2021
;
Tabone and Francalanza 2022
develop a tool that statically checks
Elixir
(
Jurić 2019
)
actors against binary session types to prove session
fidelity.

Neykova and Yoshida 2017b
propose a programming model for dynamically checking actor
communication against multiparty session types
(
Honda et al. 2016
)
, later
implemented in Erlang by
Fowler 2016
.

Neykova and Yoshida 2017a
show how causality information in global types
can support efficient recovery strategies.

Harvey et al. 2021
use multiparty session types with explicit connection
actions
(
Hu and Yoshida 2017
)
to give strong guarantees about actors that support runtime
adaptation, but an actor can only participate in one session at a time.
Using session types to structure communication requires specifying
point-to-point interactions, typically using different libraries and formalisms.
In contrast, our mailbox typing approach naturally fits idiomatic actor
programming paradigms.

Bagherzadeh and Rajan 2017
define a type system for active objects
(
de Boer et al. 2007
)

which can rule out data races; this work targets an imperative calculus and is
not validated via an implementation.

Kamburjan et al. 2016
apply session-based reasoning to a core active object
calculus where types encode remote calls and future resolutions;
communication correctness is ensured by static checks against session
automata
(
Bollig et al. 2013
)
.

Mailbox types are inspired by behavioural type systems
(
Crafa and Padovani 2017
)
for the
objective join calculus

(
Fournet and Gonthier 1996
)
.
The technique can be implemented in Java using code generation via

matching automata

(
Gerbo and Padovani 2019
)
, and dependency graphs can rule out
deadlocks
(
Padovani 2018a
)
, but the authors do not consider a programming
language design.

Scalas et al. 2019
define a behavioural type system for Scala actors. Types
are written in a domain-specific language, and type-level model checking
determines safety and liveness properties.
Their system focuses on the behaviour
of a process, rather than the state of the mailbox.

Session-typed functional languages.

Session types
(
Honda 1993
;
Honda et al. 1998
)
were originally
considered in the setting of process calculi;
Gay and Vasconcelos 2010
were first to
integrate session types in a functional language by building on the linear

\lambda
-calculus, and their approach has been adopted by several other works
(
e.g.

(
Lindley and Morris 2015
;
Almeida et al. 2022
)
).
Linear types are insufficient for mailbox typing since we require

multiple
uses of a mailbox name as a sender; we believe our
use of quasi-linearity for behavioural typing is novel, and we conjecture that it could
be used to support other paradigms (
e.g.
broadcast) that require
non-linear variable use.

Co-contextual typing.

Co-contextual typing
(
Erdweg et al. 2015
)
was originally introduced to support
efficient incremental type-checking, and has also been used to support
intrinsically-typed compilation
(
Rouvoet et al. 2021
)
.

Padovani 2014
uses a co-contextual type algorithm for the linear

\pi
-calculus with sums, products, and recursive types; and
Ciccone and Padovani 2022

use it when analysing fair termination properties.

Backwards bidirectional typing

(
Zeilberger 2015
)
is a co-contextual
formulation of bidirectional typing, and we are first to use it in a language
implementation.
Co-contextual typing has parallels with the co-de Bruijn nameless variable
representation
(
McBride 2018
)
, where subterms are annotated with the
variables they contain.

Safety via static analysis

Christakis and Sagonas 2011
implement a static analyser for Erlang that detects errors
such as receiving from an empty mailbox, payload mismatches, redundant patterns,
and orphan messages.
All of these issues can be detected with mailbox types, which also allow us
to specify the mailbox state.

Harrison 2018
implements an approach incorporating both typechecking and
static analysis to detect errors such as orphan messages and redundant patterns.

8.
Conclusion and Future Work

Concurrent and distributed applications can harbour subtle and insidious bugs,
including protocol violations and deadlocks.
Behavioural types ensure
correct-by-construction
communication-centric
software, but are difficult to apply to actor languages.
We have proposed the
first
language design incorporating
mailbox types

which characterise mailbox communication.
The multiple-writer, single-reader nature of mailbox-oriented messaging makes
the integration of mailbox types in programming languages highly challenging.
We have addressed these challenges through a novel use of quasi-linear types and
have formalised and implemented an algorithmic type system based on backwards
bidirectional typing (§
4
), proving it to be sound and
complete with respect to the declarative type system (§
3
).
Our approach can flexibly express common communication patterns (
e.g.
master-worker) and a real-world case study based on factory automation.

Future work.

We are investigating implementing mailbox types in a tool for mainstream actor
languages,
e.g.
Erlang; in parallel, we are investigating how languages with
first-class mailboxes can be compiled to standard actor languages in order to
leverage mature runtimes.
We plan to consider finer-grained inter-process alias control, and
co-contextual typing with
type
constraints (as well
as pattern constraints), enabling us to study more advanced language
features,
e.g.
polymorphism.

Acknowledgements.

We thank the anonymous ICFP and AEC reviewers for their constructive reviews; our
STARDUST colleagues for their helpful comments; and Roland Kuhn for
discussion of the case study. This work was supported by EPSRC Grant
EP/T014628/1 (STARDUST).

Appendices

Appendix Contents

Appendix A
Omitted Definitions

Here we add in the omitted definitions from the main body of the paper.

A.1.
Algorithmic environment combination operators

Environment join

\Theta_{1}\fatsemi\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi

\frac{}{\cdot\fatsemi\cdot\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\cdot;\emptyset}\par\frac{x\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{2})\quad\quad\Theta_{1}\fatsemi\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi}{\Theta_{1},x{:}\tau\fatsemi\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x{:}\tau;\Phi}\par\frac{x\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{1})\quad\quad\Theta_{1}\fatsemi\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi}{\Theta_{1}\fatsemi\Theta_{2},x{:}\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x{:}\tau;\Phi}\par\frac{\tau_{1}\fatsemi\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\sigma;\Phi_{1}\quad\Theta_{1}\fatsemi\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{2}}{\Theta_{1},x{:}\tau_{1}\fatsemi\Theta_{2},x{:}\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x{:}\sigma;\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}}

Environment merge

\Theta_{1}\sqcap\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi

\frac{}{\cdot\sqcap\cdot\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\cdot;\emptyset}\par\frac{x\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{2})\quad\Theta_{1}\sqcap\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi}{\Theta_{1},x{:}C\sqcap\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x{:}C;\Phi}\par\frac{x\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{2})\quad\Theta_{1}\sqcap\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi}{\Theta_{1},x{:}\texttt{!}\gamma^{\eta}\sqcap\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x{:}\texttt{!}(\gamma\oplus\mathbb{1})^{\eta};\Phi}\par\frac{x\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{1})\quad\Theta_{1}\sqcap\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{2}}{\Theta_{1}\sqcap\Theta_{2},x{:}C\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x{:}\tau;\Phi}\par\frac{x\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{1})\quad\Theta_{1}\sqcap\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{2}}{\Theta_{1}\sqcap\Theta_{2},x{:}\texttt{!}\gamma^{\eta}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x{:}\texttt{!}(\gamma\oplus\mathbb{1})^{\eta};\Phi}\par\frac{\tau_{1}\sqcap\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\sigma;\Phi_{1}\quad\Theta_{1}\sqcap\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{2}}{\Theta_{1},x{:}\tau_{1}\sqcap\Theta_{2},x{:}\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x{:}\sigma;\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}}

Disjoint combination

\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi

\frac{}{\cdot+\cdot\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\cdot;\emptyset}\par\frac{x\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{2})\quad\quad\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi}{\Theta_{1},x{:}\tau+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x{:}\tau;\Phi}\par\frac{x\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{1})\quad\quad\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi}{\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2},x{:}\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x{:}\tau;\Phi}\par\frac{\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi\quad}{\Theta_{1},x{:}C+\Theta_{2},x{:}C\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x{:}C;\Phi}

The environment join operator
\Theta_{1}\fatsemi\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi

concatenates
\Theta_{1}
and
\Theta_{2}
, computing the algorithmic type join of any
types for overlapping variables, and produces constraints
\Phi
.

The environment merge computes the algorithmic type merge of any overlapping
types. If a variable is in one environment but not another, then if it is a base
type, it is simply added to the output environment. If it is a send mailbox
type
\texttt{!}\gamma^{\eta}
, then its type is changed to

\texttt{!}(\gamma\oplus\mathbb{1})^{\eta}
to denote the fact that it may not
be used.

Disjoint environment combination combines two environments; if there are two
overlapping types then they must be identical base types.

Appendix B
Details of Extensions

Here we discuss the details of the extensions overviewed
in §
5
.

B.1.
Product and sum types

Product and sum types can be added relatively straightforwardly. In both cases,
their components must be returnable as otherwise it would not be possible to
substitute their contents upon deconstruction.

Product types.

We can write the declarative typing rules for products as follows; the rules are
unremarkable apart from the requirement that each component of the pair must be
returnable in the elimination form.

\frac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash V\,{:}\,A\quad\Gamma_{2}\vdash W\,{:}\,B}{\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\vdash(V,W)\,{:}\,A\times B}\quad\textsc{T-Pair}\par\frac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash V\,{:}\,A_{1}\times A_{2}\quad\textsf{returnable}(A)\quad\textsf{returnable}(B)\quad\Gamma_{2},x:A_{1},y:A_{2}\vdash V\,{:}\,B}{\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\vdash\textbf{{let}}\>(x,y){}=V\>\textbf{{in}}\>M\,{:}\,B}\quad\textsc{T-LetPair}

We can write the corresponding algorithmic rules as follows:

\frac{V\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{1};\,\Phi_{1}\hskip 9.24994ptW\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{2};\,\Phi_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{3}}{(V,W)\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\times\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}\cup\Phi_{3}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{TC-Pair}\par\frac{V\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{1};\,\Phi_{1}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsf{returnable}(\tau_{1})\hskip 9.24994pt\textsf{returnable}(\tau_{2})\hskip 9.24994ptM\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{2};\,\Phi_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\textsf{check}(\Theta_{2},x,A_{1})=\Phi_{3}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsf{check}(\Theta_{2},y,A_{2})=\Phi_{4}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{5}}{\textbf{{let}}\>(x,y){{:(\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2})}}=V\>\textbf{{in}}\>M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi_{5}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{TC-LetPair}\par\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;B\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x:\tau_{1},y:\tau_{2};\,\Theta_{1}{\Phi_{1}}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\textsf{returnable}(\tau_{1})\hskip 9.24994pt\textsf{returnable}(\tau_{2})\hskip 9.24994ptV\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{2};\,\Phi_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{3}}{\textbf{{let}}\>(x,y){}=V\>\textbf{{in}}\>M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}\cup\Phi_{3}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{TC-LetPairNoAnn}

Pair construction (
TC-Pair
) checks that both components have the given
types. Environment combination and constraints are handled as usual.
Deconstructing the pair in general requires an annotation (
TC-LetPair
); as with the let
rule, we check that the pair has the given annotation and that the types
inferred in the environment of the continuation are consistent with the
annotation.
If both components of the pair are used within the continuation then we can omit
the annotation (
TC-LetPairNoAnn
): the rule first checks that the continuation
has the given type, and inspects the resulting environment to construct the
product type used for checking
V
.

Sum types.

Sum types are similar to product types; again, the declarative rules are
unremarkable except for the requirement that sum components must be returnable
in the elimination rule.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash V\,{:}\,A}{\Gamma\vdash\textbf{{inl}}\;V\,{:}\,A+B}\quad\textsc{T-Inl}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash V\,{:}\,B}{\Gamma\vdash\textbf{{inr}}\;V\,{:}\,A+B}\quad\textsc{T-Inr}\par\frac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash V\,{:}\,A_{1}+A_{2}\quad\quad\textsf{returnable}(A)\quad\textsf{returnable}(B)\quad\Gamma_{2},x:A_{1}\vdash M\,{:}\,B\quad\Gamma_{2},y:A_{2}\vdash N\,{:}\,B}{\Gamma\vdash\textbf{{case}}\;V\;\textbf{{of}}\;\{\textbf{{inl}}\;x:A_{1}\mapsto M;\textbf{{inr}}\;y:A_{2}\mapsto N\}\,{:}\,B}\quad\textsc{T-Case}\par\frac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash V\,{:}\,A_{1}+A_{2}\quad\quad\Gamma_{2},x:A_{1}\vdash M\,{:}\,B\quad\Gamma_{2},y:A_{2}\vdash N\,{:}\,B}{\Gamma\vdash\textbf{{case}}\;V\;\textbf{{of}}\;\{\textbf{{inl}}\;x\mapsto M;\textbf{{inr}}\;y\mapsto N\}\,{:}\,B}\quad\textsc{T-CaseNoAnn}

We can also write the corresponding algorithmic rules:

\frac{V\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi}{\textbf{{inl}}\;V\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau+\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{TC-Inl}\par\frac{V\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi}{\textbf{{inr}}\;V\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau+\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{TC-Inr}\par\frac{V\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;A+B\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{1};\,\Phi_{1}\hskip 9.24994ptM\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{2};\,\Phi_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptN\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{3};\,\Phi_{3}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsf{check}(\Theta_{2},x,A)=\Phi_{4}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\textsf{check}(\Theta_{3},y,B)=\Phi_{5}\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta_{2}\mathop{-}x\sqcap\Theta_{3}\mathop{-}y\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{4};\Phi_{6}\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{4}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{7}}{\textbf{{case}}\;V\;\textbf{{of}}\;\{\textbf{{inl}}\;x:A\mapsto M;\textbf{{inr}}\;y:B\mapsto N\}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi_{7}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{TC-Case}\par\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{1},x:\tau_{1};\,\Phi_{1}\hskip 9.24994ptN\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{2},y:\tau_{2};\,\Phi_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptV\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{3};\,\Phi_{3}\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta_{1}\sqcap\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{4};\Phi_{4}\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta_{3}+\Theta_{4}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{5}}{\textbf{{case}}\;V\;\textbf{{of}}\;\{\textbf{{inl}}\;x\mapsto M;\textbf{{inr}}\;y\mapsto N\}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi_{5}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{TC-CaseNoAnn}

As expected, sum injections are checking cases; similar to the product rules, we
also have two separate rules for case expressions which allow annotations to be
elided if both
x
and
y
are used within continuations
M
and
N
.

B.2.
Contextual Type Information

The other main extension supplements co-contextual type checking with contextual
type information in order to enable extensions such as higher-order functions
and interfaces.

B.2.1.
Extended syntax

We begin by showing the modified syntax.

Syntax

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\text{Modified types}&\pi,\rho&::=&C\,\mid\,\texttt{!}^{I}\gamma\,\mid\,\texttt{?}^{I}\delta\,\mid\,\overrightarrow{\tau}\xrightarrow{\diamond}\sigma\\
\text{Interfaces}&I&::=&\cdot\,\mid\,I,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\mapsto\pi\\
\text{Linearity annotations}&\diamond&::=&\square\,\mid\,\blacksquare\\
\text{Additional values}&V,W&::=&\cdots\,\mid\,\lambda^{\!\diamond}(\overrightarrow{x:\tau}){:}\,\sigma\,.\,M\\
\text{Modified computations}&M,N&::=&\cdots\,\mid\,{V}^{\overrightarrow{\tau}\xrightarrow{}\sigma}(W)\,\mid\,\textbf{{new}}\!\texttt{[}{I}\texttt{]}\,\mid\,\textbf{{guard}}^{I}\,V\,{:}\,E\,\{\overrightarrow{G}\}\\
&&\,\mid&{V}\,{!}^{I}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{W}\texttt{]}\end{array}

Like signatures in the core calculus, interfaces
I
map message names to
lists of types.
We modify types
\pi,\rho
to include
interface-annotated

mailbox types, as well as
n
-ary function types
\overrightarrow{\tau}\xrightarrow{\diamond}\sigma

that are annotated as either
linear
(
\square
, meaning that the function
closes over linear variables and therefore must be used precisely once) or

unrestricted
(
\blacksquare
, meaning that the function only closes over
unrestricted variables and therefore can be used an unlimited number of times).

First-class functions.

We extend values with fully-annotated,
n
-ary anonymous functions. We require a
return annotation since we wish to check the body type, while also synthesising
a return type. We opt for an
n
-ary function rather than a curried
representation because anonymous functions may only close over returnable
values, to ensure they do not violate the conditions on lexical scoping
once applied.

Example B.1.

Consider the following expression:

\begin{array}[]{l}\textbf{{let}}\>\textit{mb}{}=\textbf{{new}}\>\textbf{{in}}\\
\textbf{{let}}\>f{}=(\lambda^{\!\square}(){:}\,\mathbf{1}\,.\,\textit{mb}\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\texttt{]})\>\textbf{{in}}\\
\textbf{{guard}}\,\textit{mb}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\,\{\\
\quad\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;\textit{mb}\mapsto\textbf{{free}}\;\textit{mb}\\
\}\\
f()\end{array}

Here we bind
f
to a function which sends message
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}
to mailbox

mb
; note that it is used lexically before the
guard
, which
aligns with type combination. However, after reducing the expression (assuming
that
a
is chosen as a runtime name), we obtain the following term:

\begin{array}[]{l}\textbf{{guard}}\,\textit{a}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\,\{\\
\quad\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;\textit{mb}\mapsto\textbf{{free}}\;\textit{mb}\\
\}\\
(\lambda^{\!\square}(){:}\,\mathbf{1}\,.\,\textit{mb}\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\texttt{]})()\end{array}

After substituting the function body for
f
we now have a second-class use

after
the first-class use, violating the ordering of returnable and
second-class usages.

Mailbox terms.

We extend computations so that a user specifies an interface
I
when creating a
mailbox (
\textbf{{new}}\!\texttt{[}{I}\texttt{]}
). Furthermore, we also augment send and guard expressions
with the interface of the mailbox they operate on. Unlike the annotation on

new
, this does not need to be specified by the user, but instead is added
by a straightforward type-directed translation.

B.2.2.
Type-directed translation

We propagate annotations to function application and mailbox terms via a
contextual type-directed translation.

To do so, we introduce
pre-types

P,Q
: the main difference
is that mailbox types
do not
carry a pattern, but only an interface.

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\text{Pre-types}&P,Q&::=&C\,\mid\,\textsf{Mailbox}(I)\,\mid\,\overrightarrow{P}\xrightarrow{\diamond}Q\\
\text{Pre-type environments}&\Omega&::=&\cdot\,\mid\,\Omega,x:P\end{array}

The type-directed translation pass follows the form of a standard type system
for the simply-typed
\lambda
calculus so we omit the rules here. However the
judgement has the form
\Omega\vdash M\,{:}\,P\rightsquigarrow N
which can be
read “under pre-type environment
\Omega
, term
M
has pre-type

P
and produces annotated term
N
”.

B.2.3.
Constraint generation rules

Finally, we can see how to write constraint generation rules for the extended
calculus. The rules in the declarative setting are similar.

Modified constraint generation rules

M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi

M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi

\{E;I\}\;G\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi;\,\Phi;\,F

\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime};\,\Phi_{1}\quad\Theta=\Theta^{\prime}\mathop{-}\overrightarrow{x}\quad\quad\textsf{check}(\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{\tau},\Theta^{\prime})=\Phi_{2}\quad\textsf{returnable}(\Theta)}{\lambda^{\!\square}(\overrightarrow{x:\tau}){:}\,\sigma\,.\,M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\overrightarrow{\tau}\xrightarrow{\square}\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}}\quad\textsc{TS-LinLam}\par\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime};\,\Phi_{1}\quad\Theta=\Theta^{\prime}\mathop{-}\overrightarrow{x}\quad\quad\textsf{check}(\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{\tau},\Theta^{\prime})=\Phi_{2}\quad\textsf{unr}(\Theta)\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi_{3}\quad\textsf{returnable}(\Theta)}{\lambda^{\!\blacksquare}(\overrightarrow{x:\tau}){:}\,\sigma\,.\,M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\overrightarrow{\tau}\xrightarrow{\blacksquare}\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}\cup\Phi_{3}}\quad\textsc{TS-UnLam}\par\frac{V\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\overrightarrow{\tau}\xrightarrow{\diamond}\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime};\,\Phi\quad\quad(W_{i}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau_{i}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{i};\,\Phi_{i})_{i\in 1..n}\quad\Theta^{\prime}+\Theta_{1}+\ldots+\Theta_{n}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi}{{V}^{\overrightarrow{\tau}\xrightarrow{\diamond}\sigma}(\overrightarrow{W})\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi\cup\Phi_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\Phi_{n}}\quad\textsc{TS-FnApp}\par\frac{I({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})=\overrightarrow{\pi}\quad V\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\texttt{!}^{I}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}^{\circ}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime};\,\Phi\quad\quad(W_{i}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\lceil\pi_{i}\rceil\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{i};\,\Phi^{\prime}_{i})_{i}\quad\Theta^{\prime}+\Theta_{1}+\cdots+\Theta_{n}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi^{\prime\prime}}{{V}\,{!}^{I}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{W}\texttt{]}\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\mathbf{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi\cup\Phi^{\prime}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi^{\prime}_{n}\cup\Phi^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{TS-Send}\par\frac{}{\textbf{{new}}\!\texttt{[}{I}\texttt{]}\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\texttt{?}^{I}\mathbb{1}^{{\bullet}}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\cdot;\,\emptyset}\quad\textsc{TS-New}\par\frac{\{E;I\}\;\overrightarrow{G}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi;\,\Phi_{1};\,F\quad\quad V\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\texttt{?}^{I}F^{{\bullet}}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime};\,\Phi_{2}\quad\Psi+\Theta^{\prime}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{3}}{\textbf{{guard}}^{I}\,V\,{:}\,E\,\{\overrightarrow{G}\}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}\cup\Phi_{3}\cup\{E\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}F\}}\quad\textsc{TC-Guard}\par\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime},y:\texttt{?}^{I}\gamma^{{\bullet}};\,\Phi_{1}\quad I({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})={\overrightarrow{\pi}}\quad\Theta=\Theta^{\prime}\mathop{-}\overrightarrow{x}\quad\textsf{interfaces}(\overrightarrow{\pi})\cap\textsf{interfaces}(\Theta)=\emptyset\quad\textsf{check}(\Theta^{\prime},\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{\lceil\pi\rceil})=\Phi_{2}}{\{E;I\}\;\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{x}\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;y\mapsto M\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}\cup\{E\mathop{/}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\gamma\};\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\odot(E\mathop{/}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})}\quad\textsc{TCG-Recv}

We require three new rules for first-class functions:
TS-LinLam
types a
linear anonymous function by checking that the body has the given result type,
and the inferred environment uses variables consistently with the parameter
annotations; the rule synthesises a type consistent with the annotation.
Further, we require that the inferred environment only closes over
variables with returnable types. Rule
TS-UnLam
is similar, but
additionally requires that the inferred environment is unrestricted. Rule

TS-FnApp
types an annotated function application, checking that the
function has the given annotation and that the arguments have the correct types.

As for the rules that support interfaces, rule
TS-Send
is similar but
looks up the types according to the interface rather than the global signature,
and checks that the target mailbox has the given interface. Rule
TS-New

synthesises a mailbox type with the user-supplied interface. Finally, we modify
the shape of the guard typing judgement to record the interface of the mailbox
being guarded upon, and use this to look up the desired payload types in

TCG-Recv
.

Appendix C
Supplementary Implementation and Evaluation Material

Our typechecking tool processes
Pat
programs specified in plain text files that are structured in three segments:

(1)

Interface definitions
that establish the set of messages that a mailbox can receive;

(2)

Function definitions
that specify processes that are instantiable using
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
spawn
;

(3)

Body
an invocation of a function defined in (
2
) that acts as the program entry point.

Pat
programs are type checked following the six-stage pipeline of
Figure

14
; see §
6.1
for an overview.

Figure 14
.
Pat
type checking pipeline

C.1.
Experimental Conditions

We report the mean typechecking time, excluding phases 1–3 of the pipeline.
Measurements are made on a MacBook M1 Pro with 16GB of memory, running macOS
13.2 and OCaml 5.0. To ensure minimal variability in our measurements show in

Table

1
, we preformed 1000 repetition of each experiment.
The number of repetitions was determined empirically by calculating the
coefficient of variation (CV)
(
Devore and Berk 2012
)
,
i.e.
the ratio of the standard
deviation to the mean,
\textrm{CV}=\sigma/\bar{x}
, for different repetitions
until an adequately-low value (
<\!10\%
) was obtained.

C.2.
Case Study

We give the full
Pat
program for our factory case study described in §
6.2.2
.
The interaction sequence between our different entities, Robots, Door, and Warehouse, is captured in
Figure

13
and naturally translates to the code that follows.

\lst

\lst

Door

The Door
accepts zero or more
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Want

messages, replying to each with
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Busy
or
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
GoIn
.
In the latter case, the Door informs the Warehouse of an inbound Robot by sending it a
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Prepare
message, and transitioning to the
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
busy
state (lines
36
–
39
).
Both
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
GoIn
and
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Prepare
include an updated self-reference to ensure precise types.
The
clause
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
free
on
line

35
handles the case where no Robots are present.
When
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
busy
, the Door mailbox potentially contains an
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Inside
message from the admitted Robot, a
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Prepared
message from the Warehouse,
and
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Want
messages sent by other Robots requesting access (
line

44
).
These
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Want
messages are answered with
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Busy
, as lines
45
–
47
show.
Once the Door receives the
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Inside
message, it awaits a
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Prepared
message issued by the Warehouse, before notifying the latter that the Robot is collecting its part via
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Deliver
(lines
line

48
–
51
).
Eventually, the Robot demands exit
by sending
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
WantLeave
to the Door, which handles it on lines
line

53
–
54
.
The Door transitions to the
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
ready
state, whereupon it confirms that the Robot has exited and that the Warehouse is available; these interactions are captured by the
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
Outside
and
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
TableIdle
messages respectively (lines
63
–
72
).
Finally, the Door transitions back to
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
clear
on
line

73
, ready to service other Robots.

Appendix D
Proofs

D.1.
Preservation

D.1.1.
Auxiliary Definitions and Lemmas

We extend
\textsf{returnable}(-)
to typing environments, writing

\textsf{returnable}(\Gamma)
if
\textsf{returnable}(A)
for each
x:A\in\Gamma
.
Similarly, we write
\textsf{irrelevant}(A)
if
A
is irrelevant (i.e., it is
a mailbox type
\texttt{!}E^{\eta}\leq\texttt{!}\mathbb{1}^{\eta}
), and
extend this to environments.

We write
\textsf{fv}(M)
to return the free variables of a term.

Environment subtyping includes a notion of weakening. Read top-down, environment
subtyping rules allow us to add a variable with mailbox type
\texttt{!}\mathbb{1}
,
replace a type with its subtype, and add in base types.
It is therefore useful to introduce a definition referring to the class of types
which can be added in through
T-Sub
which may not be used by a term.
We call these types
cruft
. Cruft is a refinement of
\textsf{un}(-)
since it
also encompasses types which are
subtypes
of
\texttt{!}\mathbb{1}
.

Definition D.1 (Cruft).

A type
T
is
cruft
, written
\textsf{cruft}(T)
, if either

T
is a base type, or
\textsf{irrelevant}(T)
.

A usage-aware type
T^{\eta}
is cruft if
\eta=\circ
and
T
is cruft.

It helps to define a stricter version of environment subtyping which does not
permit weakening:

Definition D.2 (Strict environment subtyping).

An environment
\Gamma
is a
strict subtype environment
of an environment

\Gamma^{\prime}
, written
\Gamma\preccurlyeq\Gamma^{\prime}
if
\Gamma\leq\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\textsf{dom}(\Gamma)=\textsf{dom}(\Gamma^{\prime})
.

We extend
\textsf{cruft}(-)
to type environments and usage-aware type environments
in the usual way.

Definition D.3 (Cruftless).

We say that an environment is
cruftless for a term

M
if

\Gamma\vdash M\,{:}\,A
and
\textsf{dom}(\Gamma)=\textsf{fv}(M)
.

Let us also use
\Pi
to range over type environments.
A crucial lemma for taming the complexity of environment subtyping is the
following, which allows us to separate the type environment required for typing
the term from the cruft introduced by environment subtyping.

Lemma D.4.

If
\Gamma\vdash M\,{:}\,A
, then there exist
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},\Pi_{3}
such
that:

•

\Gamma=\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}

•

\Pi_{3}\vdash M\,{:}\,A^{\prime}

•

\Pi_{1}
is cruftless for
M
, and
\Pi_{1}\preccurlyeq\Pi_{3}

•

A^{\prime}\leq A

•

\textsf{cruft}(\Pi_{2})

Proof.

Follows from the definition of environment subtyping: read top-down, each
application of environment subtyping will either add a variable with an
unrestricted type, or alter the type of an existing variable.
∎

The substitution lemma is only defined on disjoint environments: we should not
be substituting a name into a term where it is already free. This is ensured by
distinguishing between returnable and second-class usages of a variable: if a
variable is returnable, then we know it cannot be used within the term into
which it is being substituted. If a variable is second-class, then there will be
no applicable reduction rules which result in substitution.

Lemma D.5 (Substitution).

If:

•

\Gamma_{1},x:A\vdash M\,{:}\,B

•

\Gamma_{2}\vdash V\,{:}\,A^{\prime}

•

A^{\prime}\leq A

•

\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}
is defined

then
\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\vdash M\{V/x\}\,{:}\,B
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
\Gamma_{1},x:A\vdash M\,{:}\,B
.
∎

Lemma D.6 (Subtyping preserves reliablility / usability
(
de’Liguoro and Padovani 2018
)
).

If
A\leq B
, then:

(1)

A
reliable implies
B
reliable

(2)

B
usable implies
A
usable

Corollary D.7.

If
\Gamma_{1}\leq\Gamma_{2}
then:

(1)

\Gamma_{1}
reliable implies
\Gamma_{2}
reliable

(2)

\Gamma_{2}
usable implies
\Gamma_{2}
usable

Lemma D.8 (Balancing
(
de’Liguoro and Padovani 2018
)
).

If
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\odot F\sqsubseteq E

where
F\not\sqsubseteq\mathbb{0}
and

E\mathop{/}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}
is defined, then

F\sqsubseteq E\mathop{/}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}
.

Lemma D.9.

If
A\leq B
and
\textsf{returnable}(B)
, then
\textsf{returnable}(A)

Proof.

Follows from the fact that
{\bullet}\leq\circ
.
∎

Corollary D.10.

If
\Gamma_{1}\preccurlyeq\Gamma_{2}
and

\textsf{returnable}(\Gamma_{2})
, then

\textsf{returnable}(\Gamma_{1})
.

Lemma D.11.

If
\Gamma\vdash V\,{:}\,A
where
\textsf{returnable}(A)
and
\Gamma
is
cruftless for
V
, then
\textsf{returnable}(\Gamma)
.

Proof.

By case analysis on the derivation of
\Gamma\vdash V\,{:}\,A
.
∎

Lemma D.12.

If
(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2})\triangleright\Gamma
is defined and

\textsf{returnable}(\Pi_{1})
,
then
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma=\Pi_{1}+(\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma)
.

Proof.

Follows from the definition of usage combination:
the operation is not symmetric for returnable mailbox types, so the returnable mailbox type must be the last occurrence of that name in the combination. For base types, the definitions of combination for
\triangleright
and
+
coincide.
∎

Lemma D.13.

If
\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}
is defined, with
\Gamma_{1}
and

\Gamma_{2}
sharing only variables of base type, then

\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}
is defined.

Proof.

Immediate from the definitions.
∎

Lemma D.14 (
\triangleright
is associative).

A_{1}\triangleright(A_{2}\triangleright A_{3})\iff(A_{1}\triangleright A_{2})\triangleright A_{3}

Proof.

Follows from the fact that usage combination is associative, and that we
identify patterns up to commutativity and associativity.
∎

Extending to usage-aware type environments, we get the following corollary:

Corollary D.15.

\Gamma_{1}\triangleright(\Gamma_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{3})\iff(\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2})\triangleright\Gamma_{3}

The same result holds for runtime type environments and
\bowtie
:

Lemma D.16 (
\bowtie
is associative).

\Delta_{1}\bowtie(\Delta_{2}\bowtie\Delta_{3})\iff(\Delta_{1}\bowtie\Delta_{2})\bowtie\Delta_{3}

Proof.

Follows the same reasoning as for
\triangleright
.
∎

Lemma D.17.

The
\bowtie
operator is commutative:

\Delta_{1}\bowtie\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{2}\bowtie\Delta_{1}
.

Proof.

Follows from the fact that
\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\boxplus$}}
is commutative.
∎

Lemma D.18.

\Gamma_{1}+(\Gamma_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{3})=(\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2})\triangleright\Gamma_{3}
.

Proof.

Follows directly from the definitions.
∎

Lemma D.19.

If
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma
, then
\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma

Proof.

Follows from the definition of
\triangleright
given that
\Gamma_{1}
and

\Gamma_{2}
are disjoint.
∎

Lemma D.20.

If
\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma
, then
|{\Gamma_{1}}|\bowtie|{\Gamma_{2}}|=|{\Gamma}|
.

Proof.

Follows directly from the definitions, since
\bowtie
is more liberal than
\triangleright
.
∎

Lemma D.21.

|{\Gamma}|\bowtie\Delta=|{\lceil\Delta\rceil\triangleright\Gamma}|
.

Proof.

For each
x
such that
x:T\in|{\Gamma}|
and
x:U\in\Delta
, since

|{\Gamma}|\bowtie\Delta
is defined, we have that
T\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\boxplus$}}U
is defined. The result then follows from the definition of

\triangleright
, noting that all types in
\lceil\Delta\rceil
are usable and
therefore combinable with any other usage.
∎

Lemma D.22.

If
\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma
and
\textsf{irrelevant}(\Gamma_{1})
,
then
\Gamma_{2}\simeq\Gamma
.

Proof.

Since
\textsf{irrelevant}(\Gamma_{1})
, we have that for each
x:A
, it is
the case that
A=\texttt{!}E^{\circ}
where
E\sqsubseteq\mathbb{1}
.
∎

D.1.2.
Preservation proof

Lemma D.23 (Preservation (Equivalence)).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathcal{D}
, then

\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathcal{D}
, relying on
Lemmas
D.16
and
D.17
and
TC-Subs
.
∎

See
3.11

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{C}
.

Case
E-Let

Assumption:

\frac{\frac{\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}=\lfloor\Delta^{\prime}\rfloor\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\Gamma_{3}\triangleright\Gamma_{4}=\Gamma_{1}\hskip 7.3612pt\Gamma_{3}\vdash M\,{:}\,\lfloor T\rfloor\hskip 7.3612pt\hskip 7.3612pt\Gamma_{4},x:\lfloor T\rfloor\vdash N\,{:}\,B}{\Gamma_{1}\vdash\textbf{{let}}\>x{{:T}}=M\>\textbf{{in}}\>N\,{:}\,B}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{}{\Gamma_{2}\vdash B\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Sigma}}{\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\llparenthesis\,\textbf{{let}}\>x{{:T}}=M\>\textbf{{in}}\>N,\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}}{\Delta\vdash\llparenthesis\,\textbf{{let}}\>x{{:T}}=M\>\textbf{{in}}\>N,\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}

where

•

\Delta\leq\Delta^{\prime}

•

\lfloor\Delta^{\prime}\rfloor=\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}

•

\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{3}\triangleright\Gamma_{4}

so,
\lfloor\Delta^{\prime}\rfloor=(\Gamma_{3}\triangleright\Gamma_{4})\triangleright\Gamma_{2}
.

By Lemma
D.14
,

\lfloor\Delta^{\prime}\rfloor={\Gamma_{3}}\triangleright({\Gamma_{4}}\triangleright\Gamma_{2})
.

Recomposing:

\frac{\frac{{\Gamma_{3}}\triangleright({\Gamma_{4}}\triangleright\Gamma_{2})=\lfloor\Delta^{\prime}\rfloor\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma_{3}\vdash M\,{:}\,\lfloor T\rfloor\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\Gamma_{4},x:\lfloor T\rfloor\vdash N\,{:}\,B\hskip 7.3612pt\Gamma_{2}\vdash B\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Sigma}{(\Gamma_{4}\triangleright\Gamma_{2})\vdash\lfloor T\rfloor\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\langle x,N\rangle\cdot\Sigma}}{\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\llparenthesis\,M,\langle x,N\rangle\cdot\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}}{\Delta\vdash\llparenthesis\,M,\langle x,N\rangle\cdot\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}

Case
E-Return

Assumption:

\frac{\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}=\lfloor\Delta\rfloor\quad\Gamma_{1}\vdash V\,{:}\,A\quad\textsf{returnable}(A)\quad\frac{\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{3}\triangleright\Gamma_{4}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma_{3},x:A\vdash M\,{:}\,B\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma_{4}\vdash B\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Sigma}{\Gamma_{2}\vdash A\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\langle x,M\rangle\cdot\Sigma}}{\Delta\vdash\llparenthesis\,V,\langle x,M\rangle\cdot\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}

Environments:

•

\lfloor\Delta\rfloor=\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}

•

\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{3}\triangleright\Gamma_{4}

so,
\lfloor\Delta\rfloor=\Gamma_{1}\triangleright(\Gamma_{3}\triangleright\Gamma_{4})
.

By Lemma
D.4
, we have that there exist

\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},\Pi_{3}
such that:

•

\Gamma_{1}=\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}

•

\Pi_{3}\vdash V\,{:}\,A^{\prime}

•

\Pi_{1}
is cruftless for
V
, and
\Pi_{1}\preccurlyeq\Pi_{3}

•

A^{\prime}\leq A

•

\textsf{cruft}(\Pi_{2})

By Lemma
D.9
,
\textsf{returnable}(A^{\prime})
, and
by Lemma
D.11
,
\textsf{returnable}(\Pi_{3})
.

By Corollary
D.10
,

\textsf{returnable}(\Pi_{1})
.

By Lemma
D.5
,

\Pi_{1}+\Gamma_{3}\vdash M\{V/x\}\,{:}\,B
.

Equational reasoning on environments:

\begin{array}[]{l}\lfloor\Delta\rfloor\\
\quad=(\text{expanding})\\
\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}\\
\quad=(\text{expanding})\\
\Gamma_{1}\triangleright(\Gamma_{3}\triangleright\Gamma_{4})\\
\quad=(\text{expanding})\\
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}\triangleright(\Gamma_{3}\triangleright\Gamma_{4})\\
\quad=(\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:scomb-assoc}})\\
(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{3})\triangleright\Gamma_{4}\\
\quad=(\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:returnable-non-aliased}})\\
(\Pi_{1}+(\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{3}))\triangleright\Gamma_{4}\end{array}

Let
\Delta^{\prime}=|{(\Pi_{1}+(\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{3}))\triangleright\Gamma_{4}}|
.

By the definitions of environment subtyping,
follows that

\Pi_{1}+(\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{3})\leq\Pi_{1}+\Gamma_{3}
.

Therefore:

\frac{\frac{(\Pi_{1}+(\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{3}))\triangleright\Gamma_{4}=\lfloor\Delta^{\prime}\rfloor\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\Pi_{1}+\Gamma_{3}\vdash M\{V/x\}\,{:}\,B}{\Pi_{1}+(\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{3})\vdash M\{V/x\}\,{:}\,B}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma_{4}\vdash B\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Sigma}{\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\llparenthesis\,M\{V/x\},\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}}{\Delta\vdash\llparenthesis\,M\{V/x\},\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}

as required.

E-App

Assumption:

\frac{\lfloor\Delta\rfloor=\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}\quad\frac{\frac{\mathcal{P}(f)=\overrightarrow{A}\xrightarrow{}B\hskip 7.3612pt(\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}\vdash V_{i}\,{:}\,A_{i})_{i}}{\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}\vdash f(\overrightarrow{V})\,{:}\,B}\hskip 8.19447pt}{\Gamma_{1}\vdash f(\overrightarrow{V})\,{:}\,B}\quad\Gamma_{2}\vdash B\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Sigma}{\Delta\vdash\llparenthesis\,f(\overrightarrow{V}),\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}

\vdash\mathcal{P}

D.5
we have that
\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}\vdash M\{\overrightarrow{V}/\overrightarrow{x}\}\,{:}\,B

such
that:

\frac{a:\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}^{\circ}\vdash a\,{:}\,\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\quad\Pi^{\prime}_{3}\vdash\overrightarrow{V}\,{:}\,\overrightarrow{A}\quad\overrightarrow{A}\leq\mathcal{P}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})}{\Pi^{\prime}_{3},a:\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}^{\circ}\vdash a\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{V}\texttt{]}\,{:}\,\mathbf{1}}

\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta^{\prime}\\

|{\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\bowtie|{\Pi^{\prime}_{1},a:\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}^{\circ}}|\\

\frac{\frac{\lceil\Gamma_{1}\rceil\triangleright\Gamma_{2}=\lfloor\Delta^{\prime}\rfloor\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}\vdash M\,{:}\,\mathbf{1}}{\Gamma_{1}\vdash M\,{:}\,\mathbf{1}}}{\lceil\Gamma_{1}\rceil\vdash\textbf{{spawn}}\;M\,{:}\,\mathbf{1}}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathbf{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Sigma}{\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\llparenthesis\,\textbf{{spawn}}\;M,\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}}{\Delta\vdash\llparenthesis\,\textbf{{spawn}}\;M,\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}

\Delta^{\prime}\leq|{\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\bowtie|{\Pi_{1}}|
.

\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta^{\prime}\\
\quad=\text{(expanding)}\\
|{\lceil\Gamma_{1}\rceil\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\\
\quad=\text{(expanding)}\\
|{\lceil\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}\rceil\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\\
\quad=\text{($\Pi_{2}$ cruft, so usable)}\\
|{(\lceil\Pi_{1}\rceil,\Pi_{2})\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\\
\quad=\text{(Lemma~\ref{lem:disjoint-scomb})}\\
|{(\lceil\Pi_{1}\rceil\triangleright\Pi_{2})\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\\

\quad=\text{($\bowtie$ is commutative)}\\
|{(\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{2})}|\bowtie|{\lceil\Pi_{1}\rceil}|\\
\quad=\text{($|{-}|$ cancels $\lceil-\rceil$)}\\
|{(\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{2})}|\bowtie|{\Pi_{1}}|\end{array}

It follows that since
{\bullet}\leq\circ
and

\lfloor\Pi_{1}\rfloor\leq\Pi_{3}

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}=\lfloor|{\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\rfloor\hskip 4.0pt\frac{\cdot\vdash()\,{:}\,\mathbf{1}}{\Pi_{2}\vdash()\,{:}\,\mathbf{1}}\hskip 4.0pt\Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathbf{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Sigma}{|{\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\vdash\llparenthesis\,(),\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}\hskip 4.0pt\frac{\lfloor|{\Pi_{1}}|\rfloor\triangleright\cdot=\lfloor|{\Pi_{1}}|\rfloor\hskip 4.0pt\frac{\Pi_{3}\vdash M\,{:}\,\mathbf{1}}{\lfloor\Pi_{1}\rfloor\vdash M\,{:}\,\mathbf{1}}\hskip 4.0pt\frac{}{\cdot\vdash\mathbf{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\epsilon}}{|{\Pi_{1}}|\vdash\llparenthesis\,M,\epsilon\,\rrparenthesis}}{|{\Pi_{2}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\bowtie|{\Pi_{1}}|\vdash\llparenthesis\,(),\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis\parallel\llparenthesis\,M,\epsilon\,\rrparenthesis}}{\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\llparenthesis\,(),\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis\parallel\llparenthesis\,M,\epsilon\,\rrparenthesis}}{\Delta\vdash\llparenthesis\,(),\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis\parallel\llparenthesis\,M,\epsilon\,\rrparenthesis}

\frac{\mathcal{P}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})=\overrightarrow{U^{\prime}}\quad\textsf{un}(\Gamma_{4})\quad\Gamma_{4},\overrightarrow{x}:\overrightarrow{\lceil U^{\prime}\rceil},y:\texttt{?}F^{\prime{\bullet}}\vdash M\,{:}\,A}{\Gamma_{4}\vdash\textbf{{receive}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{x}\texttt{]}\;\textbf{{from}}\;y\mapsto M\,{:}\,A\;{::}\;({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\odot F^{\prime})}

D.5

\lceil\Delta_{2}\rceil+\Gamma_{4},a:\texttt{?}F^{\prime{\bullet}}\vdash M\{\overrightarrow{V}/\overrightarrow{x},a/y\}\,{:}\,A

Equational reasoning:

\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{1}\bowtie(\Delta_{2},a:\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})\\
\quad=\text{(expanding)}\\
|{\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\bowtie(\Delta_{2},a:\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})\\
\quad=\text{(expanding)}\\
|{(\Gamma_{3}+\Gamma_{4})\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\bowtie(\Delta_{2},a:\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})\\
\quad=\text{(expanding)}\\
|{(\Pi,a:\texttt{?}E_{\mathit{env}}^{{\bullet}}+\Gamma_{4})\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\bowtie(\Delta_{2},a:\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})\\

\quad=\text{(Lemma~\ref{lem:returnable-non-aliased})}\\
|{(\Pi+\Gamma_{4})\triangleright\Gamma_{2},a:\texttt{?}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\odot E_{\mathit{pat}})^{{\bullet}}}|\bowtie(\Delta_{2},a:\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})\\
\quad=\text{(Definition of $\bowtie$)}\\
(|{(\Pi+\Gamma_{4})\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\bowtie\Delta_{2}),a:\texttt{?}E_{\mathit{pat}}\\
\quad=\text{(Lemma~\ref{lem:pcomb-to-scomb}, since $\textsf{un}(\Gamma_{4})$)}\\
(|{\lceil\Delta_{2}\rceil\triangleright((\Pi+\Gamma_{4})\triangleright\Gamma_{2})}|),a:\texttt{?}E_{\mathit{pat}}\\
\quad=\text{(Lemma~\ref{lem:scomb-assoc})}\\
(|{(\lceil\Delta_{2}\rceil\triangleright(\Pi+\Gamma_{4}))\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|),a:\texttt{?}E_{\mathit{pat}}\\
\quad=\text{(Lemma~\ref{lem:scomb-plus-defined})}\\
(|{(\lceil\Delta_{2}\rceil+(\Pi+\Gamma_{4}))\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|),a:\texttt{?}E_{\mathit{pat}}\\
\quad=\text{($a$ disjoint from environments)}\\
|{(\lceil\Delta_{2}\rceil+(\Pi+\Gamma_{4}),a:\texttt{?}E_{\mathit{pat}}^{{\bullet}})\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|\end{array}

Let
\Delta^{\prime}=|{(\lceil\Delta_{2}\rceil+(\Pi+\Gamma_{4}),a:\texttt{?}E_{\mathit{pat}}^{{\bullet}})\triangleright\Gamma_{2}}|

Recomposing:

\frac{\frac{\lfloor\Delta\rfloor=\lceil\Delta_{2}\rceil+(\Pi+\Gamma_{4}),a:\texttt{?}E_{\mathit{pat}}^{{\bullet}}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}\hskip 8.24454pt\lceil\Delta_{2}\rceil+\Gamma_{4},a:\texttt{?}F^{\prime{\bullet}}\vdash M\{\overrightarrow{V}/\overrightarrow{x},a/y\}\,{:}\,A\hskip 8.24454pt\Gamma_{2}\vdash A\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Sigma}{\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\llparenthesis\,M\{\overrightarrow{V}/\overrightarrow{x},a/y\},\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}}{\Delta\vdash\llparenthesis\,M\{\overrightarrow{V}/\overrightarrow{x},a/y\},\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis}

as required.

Case
E-Nu

Follows immediately from the induction hypothesis.

Case
E-Par

Follows immediately from the induction hypothesis.

Case
E-Struct

Follows immediately from Lemma
D.23
and the induction
hypothesis.

∎

D.2.
Progress

Lemma D.24 (Canonical forms).

If
\cdot\vdash\mathcal{C}
then there exists some
\mathcal{D}
such that

\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}
is in canonical form.

Proof.

Follows from the structural congruence rules.
∎

Lemma D.25 (Progress (Thread Reduction)).

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{P}}\llparenthesis\,M,\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis
, then either:

•

M
is a value and
\Sigma=\epsilon
; or

•

there exists some
M^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}
such that
\llparenthesis\,M,\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis\,\longrightarrow\,\llparenthesis\,M^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}\,\rrparenthesis
; or

•

M
is a communication and concurrency construct, i.e.
new
, or

\textbf{{spawn}}\;M
, or

V\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{W}\texttt{]}
, or
\textbf{{guard}}\,V\,{:}\,E\,\{\overrightarrow{G}\}
.

Proof.

By case analysis on the derivation of

\Gamma\vdash\llparenthesis\,M,\Sigma\,\rrparenthesis
and inspection of the reduction
rules.
∎

See
3.16

Proof.

Functional reduction enjoys progress (
D.25
), and the
constructs

new
,
\textbf{{spawn}}\;M
, and
a\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{V}\texttt{]}
can all always reduce.
Therefore, the body of an irreducible thread

\llparenthesis\,M_{k},\Sigma_{k}\,\rrparenthesis
must be waiting for a message
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}
on
some name
a
.
To be waiting, name
a
must be
returnable, and therefore cannot occur free in
M_{k}
or
\Sigma_{k}
.
It cannot be the case that message
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}
for
a
is
contained in
\mathcal{M}
(in which case the configuration could reduce), so
typing ensures that
a
is free in one of the other threads. Extending this
reasoning we see that if a configuration contains irreducible non-value
threads, then
\mathcal{C}
must contain a cyclic inter-thread dependency.
∎

D.3.
Algorithmic Soundness

A solution for a set of constraints is also a solution for a subset of those
constraints.

Lemma D.26.

If
\Xi
is a solution for a constraint set

\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}
,
then
\Xi
is a solution for
\Phi_{1}
.

Proof.

Since
\Xi
is a solution for
\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}
,
it follows that
\textsf{dom}(\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
.
The result follows from the fact that
\textsf{dom}(\Phi_{1})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
.
∎

The pattern variables in an inferred environment must either occur in the type,
program, or constraint set.

Lemma D.27.

If
M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi
or

M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi
,
then
\textsf{pv}(\Theta)\subseteq\textsf{pv}(\tau)\cup\textsf{pv}(\mathcal{P})\cup\textsf{pv}(\Phi)
.

Proof.

By mutual induction on the two derivations, noting that whenever a pattern
variable is introduced fresh, it is always added to the constraint set.
∎

Application of a usable substitution preserves algorithmic subtyping
in the declarative setting.

Lemma D.28.

If
\tau\leq\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi
and

\Xi
is a usable solution of
\Phi
with
\textsf{pv}(\sigma)\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
,
then
\Xi(\tau)\leq\Xi(\sigma)
.

Proof.

By case analysis on the derivation of
\tau\leq\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi
.
∎

As a direct corollary, we can show that constraints generated by equivalence
preserve subtyping in both directions.

Corollary D.29.

If

\tau\sim\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi
and

\Xi
is a usable solution of
\Phi
with
\textsf{pv}(\sigma)\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
,
then both

\Xi(\tau)\leq\Xi(\sigma)
and

\Xi(\sigma)\leq\Xi(\tau)
.

Similarly, application of a usable substitution preserves unrestrictedness.

Lemma D.30.

If
\textsf{unr}(\tau)\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi
and
\Xi
is a usable solution of

\Phi
with
\textsf{pv}(\sigma)\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
, then
there exists some
A
such that
\textsf{un}(A)
and
A\leq\Xi(\tau)
.

Proof.

By case analysis on the derivation of
\textsf{unr}(\tau)\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi
, noting
that cases are undefined for linear types, and the result follows
straightforwardly for base types.

The only interesting case is

\textsf{unr}(\texttt{!}\gamma^{\circ})\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\mathbb{1}\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\gamma
; since
\Xi

is a usable solution, we have that
\mathbb{1}\sqsubseteq\Xi(\gamma)
and
\Xi(\gamma)\not\sqsubseteq\mathbb{0}
.

Thus, it follows that
\Xi(\gamma)\simeq\mathbb{1}
and therefore

\texttt{!}\mathbb{1}\leq\Xi(\gamma)
, noting that
\textsf{un}(\texttt{!}\mathbb{1})

as required.
∎

Again, this is straightforward to extend to environments.

Corollary D.31.

If
\Xi
is a usable solution of
\textsf{unr}(\Theta)\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi

where
\textsf{pv}(\Theta)\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
, then
there exists some
\Gamma
such that
\Gamma\leq\Xi(\Theta)
and

\textsf{un}(\Gamma)
.

If two environments are combinable, and we have a solution for the constraints
generated by their algorithmic combination, then their combination is defined.

Lemma D.32.

If
\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi
and

\Xi
is a usable solution of
\Phi

where
\textsf{pv}(\Theta_{1})\cup\textsf{pv}(\Theta_{2})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
,
then there exists some
\Gamma

such that
\Gamma\leq\Xi(\Theta_{2})
and

\Xi(\Theta_{1})+\Gamma=\Xi(\Theta)
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of

\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi
.

Case
\Theta_{1},x:\tau+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi

Assumption:

\frac{x\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{2})\quad\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi}{\Theta_{1},x:\tau+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x:\tau;\Phi}

We also assume that
\Xi
is a usable solution for
\Phi
.

By the IH, we have that there exists some
\Gamma\leq\Xi(\Theta_{2})
such that

\Xi(\Theta_{1})+\Gamma=\Xi(\Theta)
.

Since
x\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{2})
, by the definition of
+
in the declarative
setting, we have that

\Xi(\Theta_{1}),x:\Xi(\tau)+\Xi(\Theta_{2})\leq\Xi(\Theta),x:\Xi(\tau)

as required.

Case
\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2},x:\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi

Symmetric to the first case.

Case
\Theta_{1},x:\tau+\Theta_{2},x:\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi

Assumption:

\frac{\Theta_{1}+\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{1}\quad\tau\sim\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi_{2}\quad\quad\textsf{unr}(\tau)\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi_{3}\quad\textsf{unr}(\sigma)\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi_{4}}{\Theta_{1},x:\tau+\Theta_{2},x:\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta,x:\tau;\Phi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi_{4}}

We also assume that
\Xi
is a usable solution for

\Phi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi_{4}
.

By the IH, there exists some
\Gamma
such that
\Gamma\leq\Xi(\Theta_{2})
and

\Xi(\Theta_{1})+\Gamma=\Xi(\Theta)

By the definitions of
\sim
and
\textsf{unr}(-)
, and knowing that

\Xi
is a usable solution for
\Phi_{2}\cup\Phi_{3}\cup\Phi_{4}
, we have that
either
\tau=\sigma=C
for some base type
C
(in which case we can
conclude with logic similar to the previous case),
or
\tau=\texttt{!}\gamma^{\circ}
and

\sigma=\texttt{!}\delta^{\circ}
where

\Xi(\gamma),\Xi(\delta)\sqsubseteq\mathbb{1}
.

Since
\Xi
is usable, we know that

\Xi(\gamma),\Xi(\delta)\not\sqsubseteq\mathbb{0}
.
Therefore, we have that
\tau,\sigma\leq\texttt{!}\mathbb{1}^{\circ}
.

We can then show that

\Gamma,x:\Gamma,x:\Xi(\texttt{!}\tau^{\circ})\leq\Xi(\Theta_{2}),x:\Xi(\texttt{!}\sigma^{\circ})

and further that

\Xi(\Theta_{1}),x:\Xi(\texttt{!}\tau^{\circ})+\Gamma,x:\Xi(\texttt{!}\tau^{\circ})=\Xi(\Theta),x:\Xi(\texttt{!}\tau^{\circ})

as required.

∎

We can generalise the previous result to an
n
-ary combination:

Corollary D.33.

If
\Theta_{1}+\ldots+\Theta_{n}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi

where
\Xi
is a usable solution for
\Phi
such that

\textsf{pv}(\Theta_{1})\cup\cdots\cup\textsf{pv}(\Theta_{n})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
,
then there exist
(\Gamma_{i}\leq\Theta_{i})_{i}
such that

\Gamma_{1}+\ldots+\Gamma_{n}=\Xi(\Theta)
.

We now turn our attention to the relation between the algorithmic join and type
combination operators.

Lemma D.34.

If
\tau_{1}\fatsemi\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\sigma;\Phi

and
\Xi
is a usable solution of
\Phi

such that
\textsf{pv}(\tau_{1})\cup\textsf{pv}(\tau_{2})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
,
then there exist

\tau^{\prime}_{1}\leq\Xi(\tau_{1})
,

\tau^{\prime}_{2}\leq\Xi(\tau_{2})

where
\tau^{\prime}_{1}\triangleright\tau^{\prime}_{2}=\Xi(\sigma)
.

Proof.

By case analysis on the derivation of

\tau_{1}\fatsemi\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\sigma;\Phi
.

Case

\texttt{!}\gamma^{\eta_{1}}\fatsemi\texttt{!}\delta^{\eta_{2}}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\texttt{!}(\gamma\odot\delta)^{\eta_{1}\triangleright\eta_{2}};\emptyset

We can immediately conclude with

\texttt{!}(\Xi(\gamma))^{\eta_{1}}\triangleright\texttt{!}(\Xi(\delta))^{\eta_{2}}=\texttt{!}(\Xi(\gamma)\odot\Xi(\delta))^{\eta_{1}\triangleright\eta_{2}}

as required.

Case

\texttt{!}\gamma^{\eta_{1}}\fatsemi\texttt{?}\delta^{\eta_{2}}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\texttt{?}(\alpha)^{\eta_{1}\triangleright\eta_{2}};\gamma\odot\alpha\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\delta

Since
\Xi
is a solution for

\gamma\odot\alpha\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\delta
,
we have that
\Xi(\gamma\odot\alpha)\sqsubseteq\Xi(\delta)
.

By expansion of
\Xi(-)
, we have that

\Xi(\gamma)\odot\Xi(\delta)\sqsubseteq\Xi(\delta)
.

Since receive mailbox types are covariant in their patterns, we can show
that

\texttt{?}(\Xi(\gamma)\odot\Xi(\alpha))\leq\texttt{?}\Xi(\delta)

and we can conclude that

\texttt{!}(\Xi(\gamma))^{\eta_{1}}\triangleright\texttt{?}(\Xi(\gamma)\odot\Xi(\alpha))^{\eta_{2}}=\texttt{?}\Xi(\alpha)^{\eta_{1}\triangleright\eta_{2}}

as required.

Case

\texttt{?}\gamma\fatsemi\texttt{!}\delta\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\texttt{?}\alpha;\delta\odot\alpha\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\gamma

Symmetric to the previous case.

Case

\tau\fatsemi\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\tau;\Phi

Assumption:
\tau
,
\sigma
are not mailbox types and

\tau\sim\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi
.

By Lemma
D.28
,

\Xi(\tau)\simeq\Xi(\sigma)
.
Since neither type is a mailbox type we have that
\Xi(\tau)=\tau=\sigma=\Xi(\sigma)=C
for some base type
C
, as
required.

∎

We can extend this result to environments.

Lemma D.35.

If
\Theta_{1}\fatsemi\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi

and
\Xi
is a usable solution of
\Phi

such that
\textsf{pv}(\Theta_{1})\cup\textsf{pv}(\Theta_{2})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
, then there exist

\Gamma_{1}\leq\Xi(\Theta_{1})

and

\Gamma_{2}\leq\Xi(\Theta_{2})

such that

\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}=\Xi(\Theta)
.

Proof.

A direct consequence of Lemma
D.34
.
∎

Lemma D.36.

If

\tau_{1}\sqcap\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\sigma;\Phi

and
\Xi
is a usable substitution of
\Phi

such that
\textsf{pv}(\tau_{1})\cup\textsf{pv}(\tau_{2})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
, then

\Xi(\sigma)\leq\Xi(\tau_{1})

and

\Xi(\sigma)\leq\Xi(\tau_{2})
.

Proof.

By case analysis on the derivation of

\tau_{1}\sqcap\tau_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\sigma;\Phi
.

For two mailbox types
J^{\eta_{1}}
and

J^{\eta_{2}}
, since
{\bullet}\leq\circ

it is always the case that

\textsf{max}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\leq\eta_{1}
and

\textsf{max}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\leq\eta_{2}
, so therefore it suffices
to consider the non-usage-annotated merge

\pi_{1}\sqcap\pi_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\rho;\emptyset

Case

\texttt{!}\gamma\sqcap\texttt{!}\delta\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\texttt{!}(\gamma\oplus\delta);\emptyset

By appeal to the definition of
\llbracket-\rrbracket
we have that

\llbracket\Xi(\gamma)\oplus\Xi(\delta)\rrbracket=\llbracket\Xi(\gamma)\rrbracket\uplus\llbracket\Xi(\delta)\rrbracket
and therefore:

•

{\llbracket\Xi(\gamma)\rrbracket}\subseteq{\llbracket\Xi(\gamma)\oplus\Xi(\delta)\rrbracket}
; and

•

\llbracket\Xi(\delta)\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\Xi(\gamma)\oplus\Xi(\delta)\rrbracket

By the reflexivity of subtyping and the definition of pattern inclusion,
it follows that:

•

{\Xi(\gamma)}\sqsubseteq({\Xi(\gamma)\oplus\Xi(\delta)})
; and

•

\Xi(\delta)\sqsubseteq(\Xi(\gamma)\oplus\Xi(\delta))

Therefore, since output mailbox types are contravariant in their
patterns, it follows that both:

•

\texttt{!}(\Xi(\gamma)\oplus\Xi(\delta))\leq\texttt{!}(\Xi(\gamma))
; and

•

\texttt{!}(\Xi(\gamma)\oplus\Xi(\delta))\leq\texttt{!}(\Xi(\delta))

as required.

Case

\texttt{?}\gamma\sqcap\texttt{?}\delta\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\texttt{?}\alpha;\{\alpha\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\gamma,\alpha\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}\delta\}

(where
\alpha
fresh).

Since
\Xi
is a usable solution, it follows that:

•

\Xi(\alpha)\sqsubseteq\Xi(\gamma)
; and

•

\Xi(\alpha)\sqsubseteq\Xi(\delta)

Since receive mailbox types are covariant in their pattern arguments, it
follows that:

•

\texttt{?}(\Xi(\alpha))\leq\texttt{?}(\Xi(\gamma))
; and

•

\texttt{?}(\Xi(\alpha))\leq\texttt{?}(\Xi(\delta))

as required.

Case

\tau\sqcap\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\tau;\Phi

where

\tau,\sigma
are not mailbox types and

\tau\sim\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi
.

By Lemma
D.29
:

(1)

\Xi(\tau)\leq\Xi(\sigma)
; and

(2)

\Xi(\sigma)\leq\Xi(\tau)
.

By the reflexivity of subtyping we have that
\Xi(\tau)\leq\Xi(\tau)
, and for the second obligation
we can conclude with (2) as required.

∎

Lemma D.37.

If
\Theta_{1}\sqcap\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi
and

\Xi
is a usable solution of
\Phi

such that
\textsf{pv}(\Theta_{1})\cup\textsf{pv}(\Theta_{2})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
,
then

\Xi(\Theta)\leq\Xi(\Theta_{1})
and

\Xi(\Theta)\leq\Xi(\Theta_{2})
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of

\Theta_{1}\sqcap\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi
with appeal to
Lemma
D.36
.
∎

Lemma D.38 (Subpattern PNF).

If
E\vDash F
and
E\sqsubseteq F
, then
\vDash F
.

Proof.

For it to be the case that
E\vDash F
it must be the case that

F=F_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus F_{n}
where

E\vDash_{\textsf{lit}}F_{i}
for
i\in 1..n
.

It suffices to consider the case where we have some
F_{j}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}_{j}\odot F^{\prime}_{j}
where
F_{j}\not\sqsubseteq E
. In this case, the
following must hold:

\frac{F_{j}\simeq E\mathop{/}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}_{j}}{E\vDash_{\textsf{lit}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}_{j}\odot F_{j}}

and by the definition of pattern residual and the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}_{j}\not\sqsubseteq E
it must be the case that
E\mathop{/}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}_{j}\simeq\mathbb{0}
. Consequently we know that
F_{j}\simeq\mathbb{0}
.

To ensure that
\vDash F
we need to show
F\vDash F
and
therefore that
\mathbb{0}\simeq F\mathop{/}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}_{j}
, which follows
by the definition of pattern derivative as required.
∎

Algorithmic soundness relies on the following generalised result:

Lemma D.39 (Algorithmic Soundness (Generalised)).

•

If
\vdash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\Phi_{1}
and

M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{2}
where
\Xi
is a
usable solution of
\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}
and

\textsf{pv}(\tau)\cup\textsf{pv}(\mathcal{P})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
, then
\Xi(\Theta)\vdash_{\Xi(\mathcal{P})}M\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)
.

•

If
\vdash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\Phi_{1}
and

M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{2}
where
\Xi
is a
usable solution of
\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}

and
\textsf{pv}(\tau)\cup\textsf{pv}(\mathcal{P})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
, then

\Xi(\Theta)\vdash_{\Xi(\mathcal{P})}M\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)
.

•

If
\vdash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\Phi_{1}

and
\{E\}\;G\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi;\,F

where

\Xi
is a usable solution of
\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}

and
\textsf{pv}(\tau)\cup\textsf{pv}(\mathcal{P})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
, then

\Xi(\Theta)\vdash_{\Xi(\mathcal{P})}G\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)\;{::}\;F
and

E\vDash_{\textsf{lit}}F
.

•

If
\vdash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\Phi_{1}

and
\{E\}\;\overrightarrow{G}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi;\,F

where

\Xi
is a usable solution of
\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}

and
\textsf{pv}(\tau)\cup\textsf{pv}(\mathcal{P})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\Xi)
, then

\Xi(\Theta)\vdash_{\Xi(\mathcal{P})}G\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)\;{::}\;F
and

E\vDash_{\textsf{lit}}F
.

Proof.

By mutual induction on all statements. We inline our proof of statement 4
with
TC-Guard
.

We know in all cases that the solution covers the pattern variables in
the program, return type, and constraints. Therefore by
Lemma
D.27
we know that any produced environment
will contain pattern variables contained in the solution. We make use of
this fact implicitly throughout the proof.

Statement 1: Synthesis

Case
TS-Base

Assumption:

\frac{c\text{ has base type }D}{c\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;D\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\cdot;\,\emptyset}

By
T-Const
:

\frac{}{\cdot\vdash c\,{:}\,D}

noting that:

•

\textsf{un}(\cdot)

•

\Xi(c)=c

•

\Xi(D)=D

as required.

Case
TS-Unit

Similar to
TS-Base
.

Case
TS-New

Similar to
TS-Base
.

Case
TS-Spawn

Assumption:

\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\mathbf{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi}{\textbf{{spawn}}\;M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\mathbf{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\lceil\Theta\rceil;\,\Phi}

Furthermore, we assume that
\Xi
is a usable solution for
\Phi
.

By the IH (2),

\Xi(\Theta)\vdash M\,{:}\,\mathbf{1}

Recomposing by
T-Spawn
:

\frac{\Xi(\Theta)\vdash M\,{:}\,\mathbf{1}}{\lceil\Xi(\Theta)\rceil\vdash\textbf{{spawn}}\;M\,{:}\,\mathbf{1}}

as required.

Case
TS-Send

Assumption:

\frac{\mathcal{P}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})=\overrightarrow{\pi}\quad V\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}^{\circ}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime};\,\Phi\quad\quad(W_{i}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\lceil\pi_{i}\rceil\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime}_{i};\,\Phi^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in 1..n}\quad\Theta^{\prime}+\Theta^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+\Theta^{\prime}_{n}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi^{\prime\prime}}{V\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{W}\texttt{]}\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\mathbf{1}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi\cup\Phi^{\prime}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi^{\prime}_{n}\cup\Phi^{\prime\prime}}

Also, we assume
\vdash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\Phi_{\textit{prog}}
.

Furthermore, we assume that
\Xi
is a solution for

\Phi_{\textit{prog}}\cup\Phi\cup\Phi^{\prime}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi^{\prime}_{n}\cup\Phi^{\prime\prime}
.
By Lemma
D.26
, we have that
\Xi
is also a
solution for each constraint set individually.

Thus, by the IH:

•

\Xi(\Theta^{\prime})\vdash V\,{:}\,\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}^{\circ}

•

\Xi(\Theta^{\prime}_{i})\vdash W_{i}\,{:}\,\lceil\Xi(\pi_{i})\rceil
for
i\in 1..n

By Corollary
D.33
,
there exist
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Xi(\Theta^{\prime})
and

\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}\leq\Xi(\Theta^{\prime}_{i})
for
i\in 1..n
such that

\Gamma^{\prime}+\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}+\ldots+\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}=\Xi(\Theta)
.
Therefore:

\frac{\Xi(\mathcal{P})({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}})=\overrightarrow{\Xi(\pi)}\quad\frac{\Xi(\Theta^{\prime})\vdash V\,{:}\,\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}^{\circ}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash V\,{:}\,\texttt{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}^{\circ}}\quad\frac{(\Xi(\Theta^{\prime}_{i})\vdash W_{i}\,{:}\,\Xi(A_{i}))_{i\in 1..n}}{(\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}\vdash W_{i}\,{:}\,\Xi(A_{i}))_{i\in 1..n}}\quad}{\Gamma^{\prime}+\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}+\ldots+\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}\vdash V\,{!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathttbf{m}}\texttt{[}\overrightarrow{W}\texttt{]}\,{:}\,\mathbf{1}}

as required.

Case
TS-App

Assumption:

\frac{\mathcal{P}(f)={\overrightarrow{\tau}\xrightarrow{}\sigma}\quad\quad(V_{i}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau_{i}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{i};\,\Phi_{i})_{i\in 1..n}\quad\Theta_{1}+\cdots+\Theta_{n}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi}{f(\overrightarrow{V})\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi\cup\Phi_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\Phi_{n}}\quad\textsc{TS-App}

Also, we assume
\vdash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\Phi_{\textit{prog}}
.

We can also assume that there exists some
\Xi
which is a usable solution of

\Phi_{\textit{prog}}\cup\Phi_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\Phi_{n}
.

By Lemma
D.26
, we have that
\Xi
is a solution
for all
\Phi_{i}
individually.

By the IH,

\Xi(\Theta_{i})\vdash V_{i}\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau_{i})

for all
i
.

By Corollary
D.33
, there exist
(\Gamma_{i}\leq\Theta_{i})_{i\in 1..n}
such that
\Gamma_{1}+\ldots+\Gamma_{n}=\Xi(\Theta)
.

Thus by
T-Sub
and
T-App
:

\frac{\Xi(\mathcal{P}(f))={\overrightarrow{\Xi(\tau)}\xrightarrow{}\Xi(\sigma)}\quad\frac{(\Xi(\Theta_{i})\vdash V_{i}\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau_{i}))_{i\in 1..n}}{(\Gamma_{i}\vdash V_{i}\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau_{i}))_{i\in 1..n}}}{\Gamma_{1}+\cdots+\Gamma_{n}\vdash f(\overrightarrow{V})\,{:}\,\Xi(\sigma)}

as required.

Statement 2: Checking

Case
TC-Var

Assumption:

\frac{}{x\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,x:\tau;\,\emptyset}\quad\textsc{TC-Var}

By
T-Var
:

\frac{}{x:\Xi(\tau)\vdash x\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)}

as required.

Case
TC-Let

Assumption:

\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\lfloor T\rfloor\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{1};\,\Phi_{1}\quad N\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{2};\,\Phi_{2}\quad\quad\textsf{check}(\Theta_{2},x,\lfloor T\rfloor)=\Phi_{3}\quad\Theta_{1}\mathop{-}x\fatsemi\Theta_{2}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{4}}{\textbf{{let}}\>x{{:T}}=M\>\textbf{{in}}\>N\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi_{4}}\quad\textsc{TC-Let}

We also assume that we have some usable solution
\Xi
for

\Phi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Phi_{4}
, and by Lemma
D.26
,
we know that
\Xi
is a usable solution for all
\Phi_{i}

individually.

By the IH, we have that:

•

\Xi(\Theta_{1})\vdash M\,{:}\,\lfloor\Xi(T)\rfloor

•

\Xi(\Theta_{2})\vdash N\,{:}\,\Xi(\sigma)

Since
T
does not contain any type variables we have that

\Xi(\lfloor T\rfloor)=\lfloor T\rfloor
.

By Lemma
D.35
, there exist some
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
such that

\Gamma_{1}\leq\Xi(\Theta_{1}\mathop{-}x)
,

\Gamma_{2}\leq\Xi(\Theta_{2})

and
\Gamma_{1}\triangleright\Gamma_{2}=\Xi(\Theta)

By the definition of
check
, we have two subcases based on
whether
x\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{2})
:

Subcase
x\not\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{2})

In this case we have that

\textsf{unr}(\lfloor T\rfloor)\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi
.

By Lemma
D.30
, we have that

\textsf{un}(\lfloor T\rfloor)
.

Thus by
T-Let
and
T-Sub
:

\frac{\Xi(\Theta_{1})\vdash M\,{:}\,\lfloor T\rfloor\quad\frac{\frac{\Xi(\Theta_{2})\vdash N\,{:}\,\Xi(\sigma)}{\Xi(\Theta_{2}),x:\lfloor T\rfloor\vdash N\,{:}\,\Xi(\sigma)}}{\Gamma\vdash N\,{:}\,\Xi(\sigma)}}{\Xi(\Theta)\vdash\textbf{{let}}\>x{{:T}}=M\>\textbf{{in}}\>N\,{:}\,\Xi(\sigma)}

as required.

Subcase
x\in\textsf{dom}(\Theta_{2})

In this case, we have that

x:\lfloor T\rfloor\in\Theta_{2}
and

\lfloor T\rfloor\leq\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi_{1}
.

By Lemma
D.28
,

\lfloor T\rfloor\leq\Xi(\sigma)
.

Thus by
T-Let
and
T-Sub
:

\frac{\Xi(\Theta_{1})\vdash M\,{:}\,\lfloor T\rfloor\quad\frac{\Xi(\Theta_{2}),x:\lfloor T\rfloor\vdash N\,{:}\,\Xi(\sigma)}{\Gamma\vdash N\,{:}\,\Xi(\sigma)}}{\Xi(\Theta)\vdash\textbf{{let}}\>x{{:T}}=M\>\textbf{{in}}\>N\,{:}\,\Xi(\sigma)}

as required.

Case
TC-Guard

Assumption:

\frac{\frac{(\{E\}\;G_{i}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi_{i};\,\Phi_{i};\,F_{i})_{i\in 1..n}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Psi_{1}\sqcap\ldots\sqcap\Psi_{n}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi;\Phi\hskip 8.19447pt\Phi^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\in 1..n}\Phi_{i}}{\{E\}\;\overrightarrow{G}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Psi;\,\Phi\cup\Phi^{\prime};\,F_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus F_{n}}\quad M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\texttt{?}F^{{\bullet}}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta^{\prime};\,\Phi_{2}\quad\Psi+\Theta^{\prime}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi_{3}}{\textbf{{guard}}\,V\,{:}\,E\,\{\overrightarrow{G}\}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi\cup\Phi^{\prime}\cup\Phi_{2}\cup\Phi_{3}\cup\{E\mathop{\mathtt{<:}}F\}}

where
F=F_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus F_{n}
.

Since guards must be unique we know that there will be at most one

fail
branch in
\overrightarrow{G}
. Without loss of generality assume that

G_{1}=\textbf{{fail}}
(the order of guards does not matter, and the argument
is the same if there is no
fail
guard).

Let us assume without loss of generality that
n>1
(i.e.,
fail
is
not the only guard).

Thus we have that:

•

\{E\}\;\textbf{{fail}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\top;\,\emptyset;\,\mathbb{0}
(i.e.,

\Psi_{1}=\top,\Phi_{1}=\emptyset,F_{1}=\mathbb{0}
)

•

\{E\}\;G_{i}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\,\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta_{i};\,\Phi_{i};\,F_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq n

•

\Theta_{2}\sqcap\ldots\sqcap\Theta_{n}\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\Phi

By repeated use of the induction hypothesis (statement 3),
we have that

\Xi(\Theta_{i})\vdash G_{i}\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)\;{::}\;F_{i}
where
E\vDash_{\textsf{lit}}F_{i}
for

2\leq i\leq n
.

Since
F=F_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus F_{n}
and

E\vDash_{\textsf{lit}}F_{i}
for
i\in 1..n
, it follows by the
definition of pattern normal form that
E\vDash F
.

Since
\Xi
is a usable solution of the constraint set we have
that
E\sqsubseteq F
.

Now since
E\vDash F
and
E\sqsubseteq F
, by
Lemma
D.38
we have that
\vDash F
.

By Lemma
D.37
, we have that there exists some

\Gamma
such that
\Gamma\leq\Theta_{i}
for each
i
. Thus, by

T-Sub
, we can show:

\Gamma\vdash G_{i}\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)\;{::}\;F_{i}
.

Therefore, by
T-GuardSeq
we can show that

\Gamma\vdash\textbf{{fail}}\cdot G_{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot G_{n}\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)\;{::}\;F
.

By the IH (statement 2), we have that

\Xi(\Theta^{\prime})\vdash V\,{:}\,\texttt{?}F
.

By Lemma
D.32
, there exists some
\Theta^{\prime\prime}\leq\Theta^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma+\Xi(\Theta^{\prime\prime})=\Xi(\Theta)
.

Thus, we can show:

\frac{\frac{\Xi(\Theta^{\prime})\vdash V\,{:}\,\texttt{?}F^{{\bullet}}}{\Xi(\Theta^{\prime\prime})\vdash V\,{:}\,\texttt{?}F^{{\bullet}}}\quad\Gamma\vdash\textbf{{fail}}\cdot\overrightarrow{G}\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)\;{::}\;F\quad E\sqsubseteq F\quad\vDash F}{\Xi(\Theta^{\prime\prime})+{\Gamma}\vdash\textbf{{guard}}\,V\,{:}\,E\,\{\textbf{{fail}}\cdot\overrightarrow{G}\}\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)}

as required.

Case
TC-Sub

Assumption:

\frac{M\;{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0}\Rightarrow}\;\tau\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\quad\tau\leq\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Phi_{2}}{M\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.65}\Leftarrow}\;\sigma\,\mathop{\mbox{\smaller$\blacktriangleright$}}\,\Theta;\,\Phi_{1}\cup\Phi_{2}}

By the IH (statement 1),

\Xi(\Theta)\vdash M\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)
.

By Lemma
D.28
,
\Xi(\tau)\leq\Xi(\sigma)
.

Therefore by
T-Sub
:

\frac{\Xi(\Theta)\vdash M\,{:}\,\Xi(\tau)}{\Xi(\Theta)\vdash M\,{:}\,\Xi(\sigma)}

as required.

Statement 3: Guards

Note that there is no case for
TCG-Fail
